Discussion Name:
Should Sex Work Be Legal?

1. Thesis:
Buying and selling sex should be legal for sex workers and their clients.

  1.1. Con:
  Sex work is immoral and unethical.

    1.1.1. Con:
    Keeping sex work illegal reinforces discrimination based on skin colour, gender, and class.

      1.1.1.1. Con:
      Common practices of the sex industry sexualise public spaces, creating a more masculine and intimidating environment that alienates women.

        1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
        Sex work is often a part of negotiating business deals [1]. This puts businesswomen at a disadvantage as they may find it more difficult to participate than their male colleagues.

          1.1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
          In South Korea [2], business negotiations are sometimes done in brothels and karaoke bars, where sex workers provide entertainment.

          1.1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
          Sex work is a widespread feature of deal-making in China [3].

          1.1.1.1.1.3. Con:
          The majority of business conducted in a sexualised environment occurs in strip clubs, where women can – and frequently do [4] – attend.

          1.1.1.1.1.4. Con:
          This is not a reason to ban sex work but, rather, to reform gendered business practices or create stricter regulations to prevent hostile workplace environments.

        1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
        Legal red-light districts often have public and highly sexualised displays [5] of sex workers that can make a portion of the city uncomfortable to walk through at night.

          1.1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
          After Amsterdam legalised sex work in the Red Light District, illegal sex work outside of the area increased by 30% (pp. 7–8 [6]).

          1.1.1.1.2.2. Con:
          People do not have a right to not be offended [7].

        1.1.1.1.3. Con:
        Laws could be passed to minimize the presence of sex work in public spaces, similar to laws in the US that restrict the consumption of alcohol [8] in public spaces.

        1.1.1.1.4. Con:
        The suggestion that women are inherently intimidated by sexualisation is reductive and ignores the complexity of their responses to such environments (p. 1416 [9]).

      1.1.1.2. Con:
      Sex work is not inherently feminine [10] and its consumption is not exclusively masculine [11]. Thus, its legal status does not necessarily impact gender equality.

        1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
        It is estimated that nearly half of those who perform sex work in the UK are men [12].

        1.1.1.2.2. Pro:
        The assumption that sex work is performed exclusively by women reinforces patriarchal thinking and contributes to discrimination against men in sex work (p. 9 [13]).

        1.1.1.2.3. Con:
        Multiple studies have found that 80% or more of the world's sex workers are women (p. 12 [14], p. 4 [15]). Thus, even if sex work is not inherently feminine, the policies that regulate it affect more women than men (p. 9 [16]).

      1.1.1.3. Pro:
      Sex workers are disproportionately women of colour and transgender people (p. 1 [17], p. 351 [18]). The criminalisation of sex work has often been used by lawmakers to further disenfranchise these marginalised groups [19].

        1.1.1.3.1. Pro:
        The legalisation of sex work would end the use of loitering and vagrancy laws [20] by police to punish sex workers and other vulnerable minorities.

          1.1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
          The police in some jurisdications use anti-sex-work loitering laws to harass [21] minorities, such as transgender women.

          1.1.1.3.1.2. Con:
          Legalised sex work is often relegated to specific places or districts (p. 3 [22]); those working outside of these areas would remain subject to legal repercussions.

            1.1.1.3.1.2.1. Con:
            It is better that sex work be legal in some locations to provide protection for sex workers than for sex work to remain illegal everywhere.

          1.1.1.3.1.3. Con:
          Even if sex work itself were legalised, laws can still prohibit related crimes such as loitering, vagrancy, and solicitation [23], making the process only nominally legal [24].

        1.1.1.3.2. Con:
        Since legalising sex work would not eliminate illegal sex work, it would instead likely generate a two-tier system of legal and illegal sex work [25].

          1.1.1.3.2.1. Pro:
          After legalisation, an estimated 90% of the Australian commercial sex industry continued to operate outside of licensed brothels (p. 21 [26]).

          1.1.1.3.2.2. Con:
          Legalisation need not address all existing disparities to be beneficial; improving the conditions of sex workers who choose to participate in the legal system is a move in the right direction.

          1.1.1.3.2.3. Pro:
          In such a system, marginalised workers would be less able to participate in the legal industry, which would further entrench existing disparities (pp. 26, 80 [27]).

        1.1.1.3.3. Con:
        The benefits of sex work legalisation favour men in the industry over other sex workers, since enforcement practices are biased (pp. 14–15 [28]).

          1.1.1.3.3.1. Con:
          Only a small proportion [29] of sex workers are men, so the majority of the benefits from the legalisation of sex work will be of advantage to women and gender minorities.

          1.1.1.3.3.2. Con:
          Men are often forced [30] into sex work at young age. Helping them will allow for a better world, as individual protection is not a zero-sum game.

          1.1.1.3.3.3. Pro:
          Women currently account for 90% of the total arrests for sex work even though the percentage of male sex workers is generally thought to be higher than 10% (p. 244 [31]).

          1.1.1.3.3.4. Pro:
          Most of the officers responsible for enforcing sex work laws are men, which creates a structural bias towards prosecuting women over men.

            1.1.1.3.3.4.1. Pro:
            Law enforcement officials justify the high ratio of women prosecuted for sex work by pointing to the widespread use of male officers to target female sex workers (p. 244 [32]).

            1.1.1.3.3.4.2. Con:
            A high proportion of male officers in vice squads is not necessarily indicative of structural bias in enforcement, but rather of gendered police recruitment and retention policies [33].

        1.1.1.3.4. Pro:
        Sex work provides many sex workers, particularly transgender ones, with economic security (pp. 2, 12 [34]).

          1.1.1.3.4.1. Pro:
          Sex work is a low-skill, high-income job [35], and minorities have less access [36] to the prerequisite education for high-skill training [37]. Criminalising sex work cuts off one of the limited options for people to improve their financial situation.

          1.1.1.3.4.2. Con:
          For groups that face intense discrimination [38], governments have an obligation to ensure better economic opportunities [39] than sex work provides.

          1.1.1.3.4.3. Pro:
          In a survey of transgender people, 12.8% of participants reported engaging in the sex trade or trading sex for access to housing (p. 13 [40]).

      1.1.1.4. Pro:
      Sex work affords women agency over their sexuality, which has historically been controlled by men (pp. 4, 6 [41]).

        1.1.1.4.1. Con:
        Customers frequently use verbal violence that can degrade or even hurt sex workers (p. 186 [42]). Such treatment undermines their agency.

        1.1.1.4.2. Con:
        Many believe that profiting from male fantasies of female sexuality [43] does not further women's sexual emancipation, but instead overwhelmingly benefits men.

        1.1.1.4.3. Pro:
        Some forms of sex work allow women to explicitly dictate the terms under which their sexual activity occurs. Such negotiation is often absent in sexual interactions.

          1.1.1.4.3.1. Pro:
          Escorts often indicate which services they are willing to perform on their websites (p. 1 [44]).

          1.1.1.4.3.2. Con:
          Coercive manipulation is a standard practice among pimps. Under such conditions, sex workers cannot fairly negotiate their prices and conditions (pp. 187–188 [45]).

            1.1.1.4.3.2.1. Con:
            Pimps thrive because they procure [46] customers, which could be easily and safely managed by sex workers themselves in a legalised environment.

            1.1.1.4.3.2.2. Pro:
            Many sex workers, such as Shandra Woworuntu [47], have pointed out that they do not have control over their negotiations because sex traffickers and pimps control their incomes.

          1.1.1.4.3.3. Con:
          Early experiences of violence by pimps make sex workers more willing to engage in sex out of fear of being beaten (pp. 1, 3, 5 [48]).

        1.1.1.4.4. Pro:
        Sex work embraces a vision of sex that is freed from the constraints of, and presents an alternative to, more restrictive ideals of proper womanhood (p. 52, 54 [49]).

          1.1.1.4.4.1. Con:
          Women can eschew restrictive ideals of womanhood [50] and traditional views of sex [51] regardless of the legality of sex work.

          1.1.1.4.4.2. Pro:
          Women who have more sexual partners are seen as less competent and less emotionally stable [52], while promiscuity is often considered a positive trait in men [53]. Legalised sex work can encourage the re-evaluation of such assumptions.

      1.1.1.5. Con:
      Sex work reinforces a patriarchal and racist society wherein women and racial minorities are commodified and objectified.

        1.1.1.5.1. Pro:
        As the state profits from legalised sex work, it has a strong incentive to facilitate the commodification of women's bodies instead of truly acting to mitigate harm (p. 174 [54]).

          1.1.1.5.1.1. Con:
          The commodification of women's bodies creates other costs for the state, mitigating the extent to which it is likely to facilitate this harm (p. 3 [55]).

          1.1.1.5.1.2. Pro:
          The state gains revenue from sex work both directly via taxation and indirectly through the stimulation of related industries.

        1.1.1.5.2. Pro:
        Sex tourism, which is often exacerbated by legalisation, can contribute to the objectification and purchase of racialized others (p. 1 [56]). For customers, this commodification detaches women from their intrinsic value.

          1.1.1.5.2.1. Pro:
          The Thai government has promoted sexual tourism through orientalist [57] and dehumanising advertising that equivocated young Thai women with an often exoticised fruit [58] (p. 24 [59]).

          1.1.1.5.2.2. Con:
          Sex tourism is driven by the varying legal status of sex work across countries and regions. If the industry was wholly decriminalised, countries would no longer hold a competitive advantage [60] by allowing sex tourism.

        1.1.1.5.3. Con:
        Compared with the impact [61] of the advertising and entertainment industries, sex work has a minor impact on the objectification of women, as it lacks the ubiquity necessary to have a significant effect.

        1.1.1.5.4. Con:
        Sex work is not the cause of racial fetishisation [62], so it is unfair to punish sex workers by criminalising their profession to reduce such attitudes.

    1.1.2. Con:
    Immorality is not a valid argument against the legalisation of sex work, as everybody is entitled to their own moral compass.

      1.1.2.1. Con:
      Government policy should reflect the moral values of the majority [63] of the populace; if most of society believes sex work is immoral, then it should be banned.

        1.1.2.1.1. Con:
        The role of government is to promote the welfare of its citizens, and individual citizens are best placed to make choices that maximise their own happiness.

          1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
          A high proportion of sex workers have experienced abuse, as well as drug addiction [64] or financial desperation [65] (pp. 40, 44 [66]). It is disingenuous to say that their choice to perform sex work is 'maximising their happiness'.

            1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
            That some sex workers are in dire straits does not mean that sex work is not their best option. It may well be their best option [67] for maximized happiness [68], given other factors at play.

          1.1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
          Many people become sex workers because they are unable [69] to get or hold regular jobs (p. 114 [70]). Sex work provides them with an opportunity to work.

        1.1.2.1.2. Con:
        The American population is split in half [71] regarding the legalisation of sex work. Thus, the continued criminalisation of sex work does not accurately reflect Americans' views.

        1.1.2.1.3. Pro:
        It is sometimes appropriate for the will of the majority to restrict the freedom of others. For example, the will of the majority is opposed to letting paedophiles satisfy all of their desires.

          1.1.2.1.3.1. Con:
          The fact that only a small number of people support a policy does not justify violating their rights. To act otherwise enables 'tyranny of the majority [72]'.

        1.1.2.1.4. Pro:
        The will of the people [73] is the basis for government authority in democratic nations.

          1.1.2.1.4.1. Con:
          Democracy involves electing people not simply to represent the exact views of people, but to act in their best interest [74], particularly regarding issues like sex work, which are both emotive and difficult to know all the facts about.

          1.1.2.1.4.2. Pro:
          Democratic representatives should act as delegates [75] for the views of their constituency.

      1.1.2.2. Con:
      Human morality [76] is predicated upon norms that benefit society as a whole; people are not entitled to behave in harmful ways despite holding beliefs to the contrary.

        1.1.2.2.1. Con:
        Ethics vary between cultures and individuals. One could argue that butchers are immoral because they offend vegetarians, or that acting is immoral because it's akin to lying, but this would not necessarily make them so.

        1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
        Sex work has harmful effects on society [77] that transcend the morality of the act itself.

      1.1.2.3. Pro:
      The purpose of a state is to provide its people with maximum rights and freedoms [78]. This requires the state to remain neutral on the morality and safety of an individual's actions.

        1.1.2.3.1. Pro:
        Legislation should not be based on moral standards, which are constantly changing. For example, though the English Buggery Act of 1533 [79] described sodomy as 'against the will of God', such a practice is not uncommon today.

        1.1.2.3.2. Con:
        Law inherently implies morality [80]; it is impossible for the state to avoid moral judgements.

        1.1.2.3.3. Pro:
        Any attempt to base legislation on a given set of moral values would unfairly intrude on freedom of conscience [81].

        1.1.2.3.4. Con:
        Laws are ethically derived, and thus it is appropriate for society to interfere with behaviours that violate social norms [82], even where they do not cause direct harm.

    1.1.3. Pro:
    Most of the world population is in some way religious [83], and religiosity is strongly correlated with negative attitudes towards sex work (p. 1 [84]).

      1.1.3.1. Con:
      Governments should be secular and derive law without reference to religious texts or beliefs.

        1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
        The separation of church and state better allows all people to be treated equally [85], as no one set of views is privileged over another.

        1.1.3.1.2. Pro:
        As there is no way [86] to truly determine the veracity of any religion's claims, it is better not to be guided by them at all.

        1.1.3.1.3. Con:
        The separation of church and state applies only to the establishment [87] of religion; the freedom to practice religion includes the right to support political policies that correspond with one's beliefs [88].

      1.1.3.2. Con:
      A number of religions are tolerant of sex work and, in some cases, have actively supported [89] it.

        1.1.3.2.1. Pro:
        Hinduism [90] is not expressly opposed to sex work by unmarried women.

        1.1.3.2.2. Pro:
        A number of religions have historically supported sacred prostitution [91] in certain times and places.

        1.1.3.2.3. Con:
        The largest [92] religions – Christianity and Islam – do not actively support sex work. Thus, though some faiths may permit it, sex work should not be allowed on religious grounds.

      1.1.3.3. Pro:
      The Abrahamic religions consider sex work to be immoral [93].

        1.1.3.3.1. Pro:
        Sex work is expressly condemned [94] by the Bible.

        1.1.3.3.2. Pro:
        Sex work is forbidden [95] by Islam.

          1.1.3.3.2.1. Con:
          The Islamic practice of nikah mut'ah [96] (temporary marriage) enables religiously sanctioned sex work.

        1.1.3.3.3. Con:
        Although more traditional Jews remain morally opposed, sex work is legal [97] and widespread [98] in Israel.

      1.1.3.4. Pro:
      Buddhism is morally opposed to sex work (p. 3 [99]).

        1.1.3.4.1. Con:
        Despite formal opposition, Buddhism in some countries is seen to indirectly support the sex trade (pp. 3, 43 [100], p. 23 [101]).

        1.1.3.4.2. Pro:
        Sex work inflames sexual desire and promotes materialism, which are at odds with Buddhist teachings (p. 1 [102]).

      1.1.3.5. Pro:
      Most religions promote monogamy [103], which brings a number of benefits to society and cannot easily co-exist with sex work.

        1.1.3.5.1. Con:
        Monogamy often encourages women to limit their career aspirations and take on more domestic work (p. 68 [104]). Sex work is beneficial insofar as it defies such restrictive practices.

          1.1.3.5.1.1. Con:
          Monogamy does not have to, and often does not, confine women to domestic roles (p. 27 [105]).

        1.1.3.5.2. Pro:
        Legalising sex work would undermine the need for intimate relationships, such as marriages, which is undesirable because they provide financial [106] and emotional stability [107].

          1.1.3.5.2.1. Pro:
          Legalising sex work could harm the stability of families [108].

            1.1.3.5.2.1.1. Con:
            Sex work, both legal and criminalised, has existed for an incredibly long time [109] without threatening the institutions of marriage and family.

            1.1.3.5.2.1.2. Pro:
            Married men are more likely than their wives [110] to purchase sex. Therefore, the legalisation of sex work may lead to more infidelity.

          1.1.3.5.2.2. Con:
          Since the human need for intimacy is not eliminated by sexual gratification [111], the legalisation of sex work would be unlikely to interfere with the desire for intimate relationships.

            1.1.3.5.2.2.1. Pro:
            Many countries, such as the Netherlands, have widespread, legalised sex work [112] and a strong tradition of monogamous marriage [113] at the same time.

            1.1.3.5.2.2.2. Con:
            The recent rise in the number of women hiring sex workers has been attributed to a decreased need for relationships [114].

          1.1.3.5.2.3. Con:
          There are many reasons why people seek relationships apart from sex; researchers have identified 27 common reasons [115] why people stay in relationships.

            1.1.3.5.2.3.1. Con:
            Sexual satisfaction is a key element of relationship satisfaction (p. 1 [116]).

            1.1.3.5.2.3.2. Pro:
            Stable relationships promote good health [117] in participants.

          1.1.3.5.2.4. Pro:
          Societal norms hold that sexual intercourse is dangerous or reckless when performed without an emotional or personal connection.

            1.1.3.5.2.4.1. Con:
            Studies show that the need for emotional and personal connections is diminishing and that 'uncommitted sexual encounters are becoming progressively more ingrained in popular culture' (pp. 1, 4 [118]).

            1.1.3.5.2.4.2. Con:
            The fact that a belief is held by much of society does not make it true [119]. In fact, contrary to popular belief, sexual relationships without intimacy [120] can be psychologically rewarding.

            1.1.3.5.2.4.3. Pro:
            Promiscuity can increase the incidence of sexually transmitted infections (STIs) (pp. 2–3 [121]).

        1.1.3.5.3. Pro:
        By reducing sexual competition among men [122], monogamy helps reduce crime and drives economic productivity.

        1.1.3.5.4. Con:
        The social institution of monogamy is also associated with a number of harmful beliefs and practices [123], which legal sex work could help to mitigate.

    1.1.4. Pro:
    Sex work reinforces the idea that the human body itself is a resource to be bought and sold. This belief has been characteristic of oppressive and exploitative commodification [124] throughout history.

      1.1.4.1. Con:
      Treating labour, including manual labour, as a commodity is an essential element of free ex­change [125] and is the basis for determining the value of labour and services.

        1.1.4.1.1. Con:
        Unlike other forms of labour, sex work is unique in the extent to which it involves sexual contact between two persons' bodies in a high-risk environment (pp. 1, 3 [126]).

        1.1.4.1.2. Pro:
        Many sex workers conceptualise their work as one or more specific forms of physical labour (pp. 79–80 [127]).

      1.1.4.2. Pro:
      Sex work necessarily requires objectification [128] because it treats a person as an instrument or means to achieve a goal, namely sexual gratification; this violates the dignity of sex workers.

        1.1.4.2.1. Con:
        Sex workers have the right to decide for themselves what violates their dignity. Governments should not make this decision for them (p. 106 [129]).

        1.1.4.2.2. Pro:
        The sexual objectification of women has damaging effects on their mental health [130].

      1.1.4.3. Pro:
      In most cases, the body of a worker is commodified as a means to another end, such as manufacturing a product or performing a service. In sex work, access to the body of the worker is the end/product (p. 3 [131]).

        1.1.4.3.1. Con:
        Sex workers provide their clients with the service [132] of sexual gratification; access to their body is part of this service rather than the end product itself.

        1.1.4.3.2. Pro:
        Sex work inherently grants the client particular forms of control over the body of the worker (p. 15 [133]).

      1.1.4.4. Con:
      Bonding between sex workers and their clients serves to counteract the commodification of sex workers.

        1.1.4.4.1. Con:
        Any emotional bond between customer and provider is defined by commodification. As such, bonding occurs on the customer's terms and only on the promise of payment.

          1.1.4.4.1.1. Con:
          All human relationships are conditional [134], so there is no reason to single out sex work as unique in this way.

          1.1.4.4.1.2. Pro:
          Relationships between sex workers and clients are often marred [135] by their transactional nature and are rarely genuine.

        1.1.4.4.2. Pro:
        Even when a relationship is premised on financial exchange, feelings of friendship and intimacy can develop [136] over time.

        1.1.4.4.3. Pro:
        Research indicates that about one in three [137] men who buy sex crave emotional intimacy in addition to sexual satisfaction.

          1.1.4.4.3.1. Con:
          Even though clients may wish to feel like there is an emotional connection, they are usually unconcerned [138] with the actual feelings of the sex worker.

        1.1.4.4.4. Con:
        Street sex workers prefer to spend less time with each client so that they can see more customers and increase their income (p. 17 [139]).

          1.1.4.4.4.1. Con:
          Even street sex workers may have returning clients [140], and might develop a friendly relationship with them over time.

          1.1.4.4.4.2. Pro:
          Street sex workers in some studies demonstrate a preference for new clients over repeat customers (p. 98 [141]).

    1.1.5. Pro:
    Sex work promotes deceit and dishonesty.

      1.1.5.1. Pro:
      Buying and performing sex work are often kept secret from partners, friends, and family. This increases dishonesty in society and harms relationships.

        1.1.5.1.1. Pro:
        Relationships are harmed [142] whenever people lie about an important issue, especially extra-marital affairs. Lies undermine trust in a relationship, even if one party is unaware of them.

          1.1.5.1.1.1. Con:
          Extra-marital affairs are not illegal in most countries [143], and the right to lie in a relationship [144] is protected by free speech laws around the world.

        1.1.5.1.2. Con:
        People would be less likely to lie [145] about sex work if it were legal.

        1.1.5.1.3. Con:
        If the stigma associated with sex work were removed through decriminalisation or legalisation, then there would be no reason to keep it secret.

          1.1.5.1.3.1. Con:
          Many people are unlikely to divulge the details of their sex lives over privacy and security concerns regardless of social stigma (pp. 1–2 [146]).

          1.1.5.1.3.2. Con:
          Sex work is often stigmatised regardless of its legal status [147].

            1.1.5.1.3.2.1. Pro:
            The stigmatisation of sex work has continued in New Zealand despite decriminalisation (p. 28 [148]).

            1.1.5.1.3.2.2. Con:
            Reducing stigma is a slow process [149]. Therefore, it is understandable that stigma still exists in legalised contexts.

            1.1.5.1.3.2.3. Pro:
            The stigma of sex work is often linked with other forms of discrimination, predominantly classism, sexism, and anti-immigrant sentiment [150], regardless of its legal status.

              1.1.5.1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
              Working-class women are more often shamed for having sex than their middle-class peers (p. 1 [151]). This may lead them to face additional stigma in the sex work industry.

          1.1.5.1.3.3. Pro:
          Western society has become increasingly accepting of sexual openness (p. 2 [152]). Given such changes, it is possible that legalisation could rapidly destigmatise sex work.

            1.1.5.1.3.3.1. Pro:
            The sex toy market has grown into a USD $15 billion [153] industry. This indicates that many members of society are more willing to be open about sexuality, leading to the normalisation of previously taboo sexual activities.

            1.1.5.1.3.3.2. Con:
            The success of global events, such as the Adult Entertainment Expo [154] and Venus [155], which advocate sex as a normal part of the entertainment industry, demonstrates that the legalisation of sex work may have little effect on the discourse.

      1.1.5.2. Pro:
      Sex workers in Northern Ireland see an average of 210 customers each year (pp. 99–100 [156]), over half of whom are likely to be married (p. 2050 [157]). Therefore, sex workers are often complicit in adultery, a breach of promise.

        1.1.5.2.1. Con:
        Sex workers should not be punished and restricted due to the deceitful behavior of some of their clients.

        1.1.5.2.2. Pro:
        Many wedding vows include a promise to be faithful [158].

      1.1.5.3. Con:
      The legalisation of sex work would help protect both clients [159] and sex workers [160] from scams.

        1.1.5.3.1. Pro:
        In an illegal context, sex workers are often used as bait [161] for other crimes, such as blackmail or robbery.

        1.1.5.3.2. Con:
        The increased visibility and social acceptance of sex work after legalisation would enable widespread scams [162], including impostors posing as legal sex workers, phishing attacks, and credit card scams.

  1.2. Con:
  Sex work legalisation would make human trafficking and sex slavery more difficult to fight.

    1.2.1. Con:
    The legalisation of sex work would reduce the involvement of gangs [163] in sex trafficking.

      1.2.1.1. Con:
      In wealthy nations, legalised sex work is associated with an increase in overall reported crime rates (p. 34 [164]). Even if trafficking decreases after legalisation, the other harms may outweigh the benefits.

        1.2.1.1.1. Con:
        There is a difference between an increase in the number of crimes committed and an increase in the number of crimes that are reported. Legalisation may only increase the latter.

        1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
        Contemporary criminal gangs tend to specialize in multiple legal and criminal enterprises [165] simultaneously. Therefore, gaining greater profits from sex work would allow them to cross-subsidize other gang activities.

      1.2.1.2. Con:
      Similar to scenarios in which drugs have been legalised, criminal gangs would move into other activities [166] to replace [167] the lost profits from sex work, mitigating the benefits of reduced trafficking.

        1.2.1.2.1. Con:
        If moving other illegal goods were a profitable activity, criminal sex work gangs would already be doing so.

          1.2.1.2.1.1. Con:
          Many illegal gangs are already involved in a wide range of activities, including illegal guns, illicit drugs, and human trafficking [168].

        1.2.1.2.2. Pro:
        Gangs may not have entered a particular market due to factors including risk, lack of expertise, or lack of connections. Removing a substantial source of their income could incentivise them to overcome these barriers.

        1.2.1.2.3. Con:
        It is better to reduce the harm to sex workers from sex trafficking than to allow the practice to continue for fear that gangs will displace their activities to other avenues.

      1.2.1.3. Pro:
      If sex work were legalised, legitimate companies would compete with sex traffickers. They would likely outcompete illegal gangs.

        1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
        Illegal businesses have extremely high operating costs, which reduces their competitive advantage compared with legal enterprises.

          1.2.1.3.1.1. Con:
          The most significant expenditure items of pimps, such as housing and transportation, will remain unaffected by legalisation.

          1.2.1.3.1.2. Con:
          Illegal businesses save money in other areas, for example, by not paying tax.

          1.2.1.3.1.3. Pro:
          Many pimps report having significant legal expenditures (pp. 207–208 [169]).

            1.2.1.3.1.3.1. Con:
            Only 29% of pimps reported having 'legal expenses' (p. 205 [170]).

          1.2.1.3.1.4. Pro:
          Illegal business owners must often bribe [171] law enforcement to avoid prosecution (p. 33 [172]).

        1.2.1.3.2. Con:
        Such businesses could act as legal fronts for trafficking, making identification of illegal activity more difficult (p. 28 [173]).

          1.2.1.3.2.1. Con:
          Sham front businesses often waste large amounts of money and would harm the competitiveness of a business in the legal sex work industry (p. 33 [174]).

            1.2.1.3.2.1.1. Con:
            If fronts were not profitable, then they would not be so common [175]; the expense of operating a front business is small in proportion with the proceeds of illegal business.

          1.2.1.3.2.2. Pro:
          Illegal businesses often set up legal front businesses to launder their money [176] and hide their crimes.

          1.2.1.3.2.3. Pro:
          Front businesses are common in sex work in both legal and illegal contexts (p. 6 [177]).

          1.2.1.3.2.4. Con:
          It is easier [178] for law enforcement to investigate and take action against financial crimes than sex work, so legalisation would make it easier to limit the sex work black market.

        1.2.1.3.3. Con:
        The additional costs associated with legal sex work may lower profit margins, creating an incentive for gangs and sex workers to persist with illegal work (p. 83 [179]).

        1.2.1.3.4. Pro:
        Both sex workers and their clients would prefer to be involved in the legal sex work industry, as it provides superior working conditions [180] to illegal trafficking (p. 1 [181]).

      1.2.1.4. Pro:
      The regulations, licenses, and bureaucratic scrutiny associated with legal business are likely to present substantial barriers to criminal gangs.

        1.2.1.4.1. Pro:
        The legalisation and regulation of sex work may make the industry safer by driving out violent actors [182], as has occurred in places where the marijuana industry has been legalised.

        1.2.1.4.2. Con:
        Criminal gangs may be the only alternative for sex workers, even after legalisation. Banks often discriminate [183] against sex workers [184], leaving them with few other means of looking after their income.

        1.2.1.4.3. Pro:
        Bringing money that was previously part of a black market into the legal system may make it easier [185] for anti-money-laundering operations to spot patterns that indicate fraudulent behaviour.

    1.2.2. Con:
    Legalisation of sex work would likely lead to the creation of new regulations and rules, which would make it easier for authorities to identify cases of human trafficking.

      1.2.2.1. Con:
      German prosecutors have noted that the legalisation of sex work has made it more difficult to prosecute human trafficking [186] and pimping.

      1.2.2.2. Pro:
      Where sex work is criminalised, the law often conflates sex trafficking and sex work, which makes the problem more difficult to address (p. 202 [187]).

        1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
        The legalisation of sex work would provide an incentive to create specific and accurate international laws to properly distinguish between sex work and trafficking (pp. 2, 15 [188]).

        1.2.2.2.2. Pro:
        US laws intended to combat online sex trafficking have also allowed authorities to prosecute websites for facilitating sex work [189].

        1.2.2.2.3. Con:
        Legalising sex work while keeping sex trafficking illegal would require additional resources to investigate the latter, as it is more difficult to identify [190] and victims are often hesitant to come forward [191].

      1.2.2.3. Pro:
      Legal sex work can require workers and businesses to undergo licensing, inspections, and registration, which would make trafficking more likely to be uncovered.

        1.2.2.3.1. Pro:
        A disproportionate number of trafficking victims are immigrants [192]; if sex work were legal, it would be easier for them to come forward when they need help.

          1.2.2.3.1.1. Con:
          Registration requirements could pose a serious issue for undocumented sex workers [193], who could risk deportation if they make themselves known to the authorities.

          1.2.2.3.1.2. Pro:
          The legalisation of sex work could enable immigrant sex workers to more easily obtain working visas without concealing their occupation (p. 143, 156 [194]).

        1.2.2.3.2. Pro:
        Legal sex workers in Nevada must obtain a permit from the police, which requires the verification of a great deal of background information [195], including birth certificates, fingerprints, and employment and residential histories.

        1.2.2.3.3. Con:
        Traffickers already rely heavily upon institutional corruption to facilitate and hide their operations (p. 2 [196]); the legalisation of sex work will not remove the bad actors who facilitate trafficking.

        1.2.2.3.4. Con:
        Legalising the core business of sex work moves sex traffickers one step closer to legitimacy, creating additional financial incentives to comply with regulations or appear to do so.

      1.2.2.4. Con:
      Those involved in trafficking are unlikely to follow the rules and regulations accompanying legalisation and will likely continue to operate illegally [197].

        1.2.2.4.1. Pro:
        Trafficking is already a serious offence in many jurisdictions (p. 1 [198]); those willing to take such risks are unlikely to be deterred by additional regulations.

        1.2.2.4.2. Con:
        To address the high proportion of trafficked workers in the industry, San Francisco has tightened regulations on massage businesses and revoked the permits of those unwilling to comply (pp. 59–60 [199]).

    1.2.3. Pro:
    Evidence shows that the legalisation [200] of sex work does not decrease human trafficking or sex slavery, but rather may increase [201] it (pp. 3, 25 [202]).

      1.2.3.1. Pro:
      In most of the countries which have legalised sex work, there has been a persistent or even increased prevalence of child sex work (p. 5 [203]).

        1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
        Reports on India's sex industry have confirmed the continued existence of many child sex workers [204] despite legalisation [205].

        1.2.3.1.2. Con:
        Pursuing customers involved in consensual sex with adults diverts police resources from pursuing sex trafficking, under-age sex work, and other crimes.

        1.2.3.1.3. Con:
        The criminalisation of sex work prevents underage sex workers from going to the police. For example, many states in the US prosecute children who are victims of sex trafficking (p. 21 [206]).

        1.2.3.1.4. Pro:
        In countries where the age of consent [207] is low, legalising sex work would enable teenagers to enter the industry (p. 39 [208]).

          1.2.3.1.4.1. Pro:
          According to the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, the participation of those under 18 years old in sex work constitutes exploitation and abuse (pp. 2, 10 [209]).

          1.2.3.1.4.2. Pro:
          Thirty-one percent of interviewed sex workers in New Zealand entered the industry before the legal age of 18 [210].

          1.2.3.1.4.3. Con:
          The laws that legalise sex work could establish an age limit [211] higher than the age of consent to block [212] teenagers from entering the industry.

            1.2.3.1.4.3.1. Con:
            In countries where sex work is legal and teenagers are considered capable of consent, there is little incentive to establish a different age of consent for sex workers.

            1.2.3.1.4.3.2. Pro:
            The age of legal consent [213] in Nevada is 16, but legal sex workers are required to be at least 21 [214].

        1.2.3.1.5. Pro:
        Children are trafficked into Denmark [215] for sex work despite it being legal for adults [216] to work in the industry.

      1.2.3.2. Con:
      There is compelling evidence that, in areas where sex work has been legalised, the majority of sex workers choose the profession out of their own volition.

        1.2.3.2.1. Con:
        The Dutch police estimate that 60–70% of sex workers are coerced into the profession because they have no other employment options (p. 38 [217]).

          1.2.3.2.1.1. Con:
          The pressure created by a lack of employment options is different from forcible coercion. Otherwise, anyone working a menial job due to lack of opportunities could be seen as being coerced.

        1.2.3.2.2. Pro:
        In Amsterdam, most sex workers, including those from abroad, are not coerced but work voluntarily and send money home [218].

        1.2.3.2.3. Pro:
        The Nevada system of legalised sex work draws clear distinctions between voluntary and involuntary sex work and creates a number of protections for voluntary workers (pp. 208, 210 [219]).

      1.2.3.3. Con:
      Legalisation can reduce the demand for trafficked women, as legal sex workers are favoured by clients over trafficked ones (p. 25 [220]).

        1.2.3.3.1. Con:
        The reduced demand for trafficking associated with the legalisation of sex work has less effect on the prevalence of trafficking than the corresponding expansion of the market does; overall, the prevalence of trafficking increases with legalisation (p. 25 [221]).

        1.2.3.3.2. Pro:
        If sex work were legal, potential clients would have little reason to visit the kind of spaces where illegally trafficked sex workers are advertised, which could reduce the demand for trafficking.

      1.2.3.4. Pro:
      Reducing the risk associated with sex work through legalisation would increase client demand, thus making trafficking harder to identify and prosecute, as supply would increase to match demand (pp. 113–114 [222]).

        1.2.3.4.1. Con:
        The mythologisation of trafficking as an integral aspect of sex work often leads actors to inflate estimates of those who are trafficked, making it seem as if more sex workers are victims of trafficking than in reality (pp. 1347–1348 [223]).

          1.2.3.4.1.1. Con:
          In 2018 alone, UN member states successfully identified 49,032 victims of human trafficking, half of whom were sexually exploited (pp. 10, 29 [224]).

          1.2.3.4.1.2. Con:
          Even after sex work was legalised, 40% of sex workers in the Netherlands were forced into sex work or experienced sexual abuse (p. 35 [225]).

          1.2.3.4.1.3. Pro:
          The scale of trafficking is often overestimated by police and researchers, and then sensationalised by media outlets [226] in illegal and decriminalized contexts alike.

          1.2.3.4.1.4. Pro:
          Even in countries where illegal sex work remains common, such as Thailand, some reports indicate that human trafficking has been declining (p. 57 [227]).

          1.2.3.4.1.5. Pro:
          Due to contradictory uses of the term [228] 'trafficking', many statistics count sex workers who have voluntarily moved to a new country as victims of trafficking (p. 5 [229]).

        1.2.3.4.2. Pro:
        Forced labour happens in many legal marketplaces, for example, in the seasonal farm labour business [230]. There is no reason to believe that legalisation will significantly reduce trafficking.

        1.2.3.4.3. Con:
        Places where sex work clients are criminalised do not display reduced rates of trafficking [231] or lower overall levels of sex work.

        1.2.3.4.4. Pro:
        So long as there is ongoing demand [232] for something that the legal sex work market cannot supply, legalising sex work would not have a significant impact on trafficking overall.

      1.2.3.5. Pro:
      Government efforts to curb human trafficking and sex slavery have been inadequate [233] when sex work is illegal, so legalisation is likely to further impede such efforts.

        1.2.3.5.1. Pro:
        The existence of numerous small anti-trafficking projects around the world has prevented the pooling of funds into a larger, focussed global initiative capable of better addressing the issue (p. 109 [234]).

        1.2.3.5.2. Con:
        The legalisation of sex work would help countries to adequately address sex trafficking by disentangling the two issues, making it easier to identify the scope of trafficking and apply resources where they are needed (p. 15 [235]).

      1.2.3.6. Con:
      Sex workers and their clients [236] may be more likely to report [237] instances of human trafficking when they know it will not get them into trouble.

        1.2.3.6.1. Pro:
        When sex work is criminalised, sex workers can face forced detention and the erosion of their civil liberties when called to the stand as witnesses (p. 4 [238]).

        1.2.3.6.2. Con:
        Even in places where sex work is legal, such as India, witnesses are often loath to come forward due to fear of retaliation by traffickers (pp. 4, 13 [239]).

  1.3. Pro:
  The legalisation of sex work would create economic benefits.

    1.3.1. Pro:
    Legalisation would reduce the cost of law enforcement – which can be quite significant (p. 775 [240]) – thereby increasing the amount of tax revenue available to be spent on other government projects.

      1.3.1.1. Con:
      Reducing spending on law enforcement should not be a goal if it comes at the cost of protecting society and individuals.

        1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
        Reducing police spending can harm communities; for example, austerity measures led to the slashing of UK police budgets, to the detriment [241] of society.

        1.3.1.1.2. Pro:
        If people feel that the police cannot provide justice for their communities, they may take vigilante [242] action. This is likely to be particularly detrimental [243] for sex workers, who are already more vulnerable.

        1.3.1.1.3. Con:
        Reducing expenditures in one aspect of policing does not mean that the overall budget will be reduced. Rather, funding could be freed up to tackle overlooked [244] areas of crime.

      1.3.1.2. Pro:
      Reducing the cost of law enforcement in one domain would allow the savings to be used to better protect society and individuals from other crimes.

        1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
        Law enforcement agencies could benefit from the allocation of additional resources to preventative strategies [245].

        1.3.1.2.2. Con:
        There is little reason to believe that such savings would go towards crime prevention. Cities and states often invest money in areas with little social value, such as tax cuts [246] for the rich.

      1.3.1.3. Con:
      Since the need to police illegal sex work will remain [247], and there will be new costs associated with the enforcement of legal sex work, the total cost of law enforcement may rise.

        1.3.1.3.1. Pro:
        Despite legalisation, Australian law enforcement and service providers often struggle to access brothels to enforce regulatory compliance (p. 2 [248]).

        1.3.1.3.2. Pro:
        The Dutch government has noted that a large amount of police capacity is used for inspections of legal sex work environments, reducing their capacity to monitor and investigate illegal sex work (p. 11 [249]).

        1.3.1.3.3. Con:
        New Zealand, where sex work is decriminalised, has not needed to increase its police budget [250] to account for sex-work-related crimes.

      1.3.1.4. Pro:
      A single sex work arrest costs US cities an average of USD $2,000 [251].

        1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
        It is estimated that the legalisation of sex work in Washington, DC would save the city over USD $20,000 per sex worker every year in law enforcement and legal costs (p. 1 [252]).

        1.3.1.4.2. Con:
        The purposes of law enforcement are to promote public safety and uphold the rule of law [253]; cost is not a primary concern.

    1.3.2. Con:
    Complying with regulations would create extra burdens [254] for the majority of sex workers, who work for themselves, so they might be unwilling to comply.

      1.3.2.1. Pro:
      There is strong evidence that sex workers do not take advantage [255] of newly legal opportunities or register with the state when sex work is legalised (pp. 3–4 [256]).

        1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
        The administrative burdens [257] accompanying legalisation, including registration and licencing fees [258], might be beyond the means of some sex workers.

          1.3.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          A sex work service provider license in Australia can cost over AUD $1,000 [259].

        1.3.2.1.2. Con:
        Sex workers could legally operate as dependent or independently self-employed persons without being formally registered (pp. 15–16 [260]).

          1.3.2.1.2.1. Pro:
          Since the 2022 decriminalisation of sex work, Belgium has instituted a system whereby the employment rights of both self-employed and contract sex workers [261] can be protected.

          1.3.2.1.2.2. Con:
          In the Netherlands, self-employed sex workers are still required to register [262] as such with the state.

      1.3.2.2. Pro:
      For legal sex workers to make use of their rights, they must interact with the government in a non-anonymous fashion, which makes them more vulnerable to abuse [263].

        1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
        The regulation of legal sex work, rather than complete decriminalisation [264], is contrary to the wishes of those sex workers who wish to remain undocumented.

        1.3.2.2.2. Con:
        The criminalisation of sex work systematically contributes to the abuse of sex workers by both state and non-state actors (pp. 1–2 [265]).

        1.3.2.2.3. Pro:
        So long as the social stigma surrounding sex work continues, any registration of occupation by a sex worker can be used as a tool of blackmail, control, or public condemnation.

      1.3.2.3. Con:
      Given all the benefits of legalisation, such as the protection of their employment rights, sex workers are likely to comply with regulations (pp. 1–2 [266]).

        1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
        If sex work were legal, sex workers could access workers' rights and employment law protections [267], which would allow them to sue for withheld wages [268], among other things [269].

          1.3.2.3.1.1. Con:
          A German study on the effects of legalisation showed that attempts to provide sex workers with a safe working environment had been unsuccessful (pp. 62–63 [270]).

            1.3.2.3.1.1.1. Pro:
            In Germany, working conditions for sex workers have worsened [271] since legalisation, as salaries decreased and working hours were prolonged.

            1.3.2.3.1.1.2. Con:
            The same study acknowledged that the German implementation was 'a very limited approach', with the federal government noting that 'a more broad-based approach to regulating prostitution is required' (pp. 79–80 [272]).

          1.3.2.3.1.2. Con:
          Sex workers who take legal action against their employers may find themselves exposed [273] by tribunals who refuse to proffer them anonymity when pursuing their claims.

            1.3.2.3.1.2.1. Pro:
            Employment tribunals place a high value on the open administration of justice [274]; legal sex work should be subject to the same laws as other industries.

            1.3.2.3.1.2.2. Con:
            Prior tribunals have found that a person's sexual activity is deserving of a high degree of privacy [275], so employment claims involving sex work may remain sealed.

          1.3.2.3.1.3. Pro:
          The legalisation of sex work is necessary [276] to ensure that sex workers can access employment stability and escape from precarious positions that leave them in debt.

          1.3.2.3.1.4. Pro:
          After legalisation, sex workers could sue employers who do not provide a safe working environment [277], giving them access to financial recompense that they are otherwise denied.

        1.3.2.3.2. Con:
        Even if sex work were legal, employers may still discriminate against sex workers (p. 883 [278]). Therefore, the material benefits of legalisation to sex workers would be marginal.

          1.3.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          Courts have upheld the rights of employers to select workers based on restricted characteristics where these constitute bona fide occupational qualifications (pp. 31–32 [279]).

          1.3.2.3.2.2. Con:
          Existing anti-discrimination legislation in some jurisdictions may be applied [280] to protect sex workers from employment discrimination, allowing them to access fully the benefits of legalising sex work.

          1.3.2.3.2.3. Con:
          Employment practices that rely on gender stereotypes [281], such as propriety, have been found to be discriminatory in some jurisdictions.

          1.3.2.3.2.4. Pro:
          In the US, for example, prior employment is not a protected category [282] in anti-discrimination hiring laws, so employers can legally use a history of sex work to discriminate in their hiring decisions.

          1.3.2.3.2.5. Pro:
          Although discrimination against sex worker employees is forbidden in New Zealand, most sex workers are not considered employees, [283] so they are not legally protected.

        1.3.2.3.3. Pro:
        Legalisation would allow sex workers to more easily unionise and form advocacy groups to protect their interests (p. 1 [284]).

          1.3.2.3.3.1. Con:
          Given that union-busting [285] is common [286], the consolidation of the sex industry into organised, legal businesses is likely to harm the negotiating power of individual sex workers.

            1.3.2.3.3.1.1. Pro:
            New regulations which impose costs on businesses often create barriers to entry for new companies and independent operators and encourage market consolidation [287].

            1.3.2.3.3.1.2. Pro:
            Due to economies of scale [288], it is easier for large businesses to comply with regulations. This is likely to crowd out smaller, worker-owned sex work firms and strengthen larger companies.

            1.3.2.3.3.1.3. Con:
            So long as sex work remains illegal, the labour power of sex workers [289] will be virtually non-existent.

          1.3.2.3.3.2. Pro:
          In the Netherlands, sex workers have established support groups, including The Red Thread and the Prostitution Information Center [290], that have helped to improve working conditions [291] (p. 2 [292]).

          1.3.2.3.3.3. Con:
          Given the existing stigma on sex workers, their unions may be marginalised [293] by regulators and law enforcement regardless of legalisation.

            1.3.2.3.3.3.1. Con:
            In the US, the right of workers to unionise [294] is protected by the National Labor Relations Act (NLRA) and the First Amendment of the US Constitution.

          1.3.2.3.3.4. Con:
          In San Francisco, BAYSWAN [295] (Bay Area Sex Worker Advocacy Network) has had success educating the public and improving working conditions for sex workers despite sex work being illegal.

          1.3.2.3.3.5. Pro:
          Since secrecy is essential in an illegal context, sex work advocacy organisations often lack shared spaces [296] for working and discussion.

        1.3.2.3.4. Con:
        Sex workers are unlikely to comply with regulations involving registration, as social stigma necessitates keeping their occupation secret (p. 10 [297]).

      1.3.2.4. Con:
      Small businesses and proprietorships are also subject to numerous regulations and requirements [298], yet compliance is in the best interest of business owners, who have a profit incentive [299] for following the rules.

    1.3.3. Con:
    The legalisation of sex work could reinforce class-based inequalities.

      1.3.3.1. Con:
      Legalised sex work would offer people a way to earn income that does not require education or training.

        1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
        Sex workers in metropolitan areas can earn extremely high incomes [300].

        1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
        Sex work is often a flexible form of work [301] that can be done in conjunction with education, childcare, and other work.

        1.3.3.1.3. Con:
        High-paying sex work opportunities may divert people from acquiring education or training that could lead to a more sustainable career.

          1.3.3.1.3.1. Con:
          Becoming a pimp is a later career option for those who enter the sex industry (p. 20 [302]).

            1.3.3.1.3.1.1. Con:
            Many people may not have the skills and resources necessary to set up and run such a business.

            1.3.3.1.3.1.2. Pro:
            Sex workers gain valuable work experience and skills, such as business savvy or proficiency in customer relations, that they can use in other careers [303] within the sex work industry or elsewhere.

          1.3.3.1.3.2. Con:
          Many sex workers use their earnings to finance [304] their education.

          1.3.3.1.3.3. Pro:
          Sex work makes for a poor career, as there is little demand [305] for older or even middle-aged sex workers. Thus, sex workers are doubly harmed as stigma keeps them in the industry and ageism ultimately ends their careers.

            1.3.3.1.3.3.1. Con:
            A lot of people desire [306] to have sex with people that deviate [307] from beauty norms, such as older people [308]. Thus, sex work may remain a viable [309] long-term career option for some sex workers.

            1.3.3.1.3.3.2. Pro:
            A study conducted in Indonesia found that the price men are willing to pay for sex drops rapidly [310] for sex workers over 25 years old.

            1.3.3.1.3.3.3. Con:
            In some countries, women performing sex work generally maintain a higher income [311], even as they get older, than their non-sex-worker counterparts.

      1.3.3.2. Con:
      The illegal status of sex work allows police and the state to oppress and harm poorer people (p. 810 [312]). Legalisation would remediate such inequalities in the existing industry.

        1.3.3.2.1. Con:
        Poorer and more vulnerable sex workers would still face discrimination under legalisation (p. 5 [313]).

          1.3.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          Despite end-demand sex work legalisation, immigrant sex workers in Vancouver, Canada, nonetheless reported police discrimination (p. 1 [314]).

          1.3.3.2.1.2. Con:
          Legal sex workers could sue police officers [315] who discriminate against them or treat them unfairly.

        1.3.3.2.2. Pro:
        The police routinely arrest streetwalkers and raid massage parlours in poor and mixed-income neighbourhoods, yet they tend to leave high-end, independent escorts alone [316].

      1.3.3.3. Pro:
      Legal sex work companies are likely to institute standardisation and quality control procedures, requiring sex workers to undergo additional job training [317]. These could exacerbate class differences.

        1.3.3.3.1. Con:
        Sex work advocacy groups support standardized training to help improve the occupational health and safety of sex workers (p. 16 [318]).

        1.3.3.3.2. Pro:
        Legalisation will facilitate the creation of sex work corporations, who are likely to support standardized training in support of their brand (pp. 95–96, 101 [319]).

        1.3.3.3.3. Con:
        There is evidence that training programs can promote solidarity amongst sex workers and help them to transcend cultural and class differences (pp. 134, 138 [320]).

        1.3.3.3.4. Pro:
        The professionalisation of sex work may follow the same trend as other jobs, with working-class people falling behind [321] their peers in roles that require more education or training.

          1.3.3.3.4.1. Pro:
          Sex workers from working-class backgrounds may be deemed less well trained or unintelligent (p. 1 [322]), making them less likely to succeed in professional settings.

          1.3.3.3.4.2. Con:
          The cost of on-the-job training is unlikely to be borne by sex workers, as it would benefit [323] employers to pay for it and have more skilled employees.

      1.3.3.4. Pro:
      Parental sex work is damaging to children, producing a cycle of poverty (p. 1 [324]). The children of sex workers often become sex workers as adults [325] because it is very difficult to break out of this cycle.

        1.3.3.4.1. Pro:
        Sex workers with children can be absent for extended periods of time whilst working (p. 5 [326]), so their children often have less parental guidance.

          1.3.3.4.1.1. Pro:
          The children of sex workers are often more vulnerable to depression and drug use (p. 26 [327]).

          1.3.3.4.1.2. Pro:
          Legal sex workers may be required to work a predetermined schedule and can be limited to working during the operating hours of an establishment (pp. 20, 114, 116 [328]).

          1.3.3.4.1.3. Con:
          When sex work is criminalised, sex workers may be separated from their children due to imprisonment or the state seizing their children – situations made less likely by legalisation (p. 16 [329]).

        1.3.3.4.2. Con:
        Legalisation could help break the intergenerational cycle of abandonment [330] associated with illegal sex work by eradicating prison sentences [331] for convicted sex workers.

        1.3.3.4.3. Con:
        Although poverty often pushes [332] people into performing sex work, the criminalisation of the industry keeps them poor [333], as criminal charges can have significant financial penalties.

        1.3.3.4.4. Pro:
        School enrolment rates [334] are low amongst the children of sex workers.

    1.3.4. Pro:
    If a country were to tax the sex work industry, they could increase their tax base [335] and government revenue.

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      An increase in the disposable income of sex workers and those working in related industries would lead to more spending in other sectors of the economy, stimulating wider economic growth.

        1.3.4.1.1. Con:
        Legalising sex work would decrease the prices [336] set by sex workers as barriers to the industry decrease. This would harm sex workers' earning potential.

          1.3.4.1.1.1. Pro:
          Lower prices may force sex workers to take on more customers or perform acts they are not comfortable with to maintain their income (pp. 18–19 [337]).

          1.3.4.1.1.2. Pro:
          Potential sex workers who were deterred by the threat of prosecution will likely enter the market after legalisation, increasing the supply of workers and reducing wages (p. 8 [338]).

          1.3.4.1.1.3. Con:
          Legal sex workers would likely leave the industry if their salaries decreased untenably; this decrease in supply would push prices upward, ultimately resulting in a wage equilibrium [339].

        1.3.4.1.2. Pro:
        Legalised sex work would lessen the need for pimps or brothels, allowing sex workers to keep more of what they charge, even as their prices decrease.

          1.3.4.1.2.1. Con:
          If sex work were legalised, pimps and brothels would still be widespread because they provide valuable services, as seen in the Netherlands [340].

          1.3.4.1.2.2. Pro:
          It is standard practice for pimps to collect [341] a part of sex workers' earnings. This could be curtailed should sex work be legalised.

        1.3.4.1.3. Pro:
        With greater buying power [342], sex workers are likely to spend more, increasing the amount of value added tax (VAT) [343] they pay to the state.

        1.3.4.1.4. Con:
        The increased income of sex workers is likely to be largely diverted [344] from related industries, such as pornography or stripping, and as such would not constitute growth.

      1.3.4.2. Con:
      Increasing the size of a nation's tax base is not good in and of itself; the morality of how the revenue is generated should also be considered.

        1.3.4.2.1. Pro:
        Economic benefits should not be prioritised over human welfare [345].

        1.3.4.2.2. Con:
        Taxation of sex work is no more immoral than any other form of enforced taxation, such as income tax [346].

        1.3.4.2.3. Pro:
        Although the legalisation of cocaine in the US could bring in over USD $12 billion per year [347] in tax revenue, the social ills associated with usage and sales of the drug outweigh the possible financial benefit (p. 8 [348]).

      1.3.4.3. Con:
      Legalising sex work may be a false economy [349] if doing so increases other government expenses in the long term.

        1.3.4.3.1. Pro:
        Directing the money spent on sex work into other, currently legal, markets would also create jobs; sex work is more likely to displace [350] economic activity than create it.

        1.3.4.3.2. Con:
        Legalising the sex work market would create new jobs across the economy – for example, in tourism [351] – thereby increasing the amount of tax paid to the state.

          1.3.4.3.2.1. Pro:
          If sex work were legal, customers who currently travel to purchase sex in countries where it is legalised would likely spend their money at home instead, fuelling the local economy (p. 8 [352]).

          1.3.4.3.2.2. Con:
          The new jobs that would be created by the legalisation of sex work might not be open to current sex workers with criminal records (p. 1 [353]).

      1.3.4.4. Pro:
      There are over 40 million sex workers in the world (p. 5 [354]); taxation of the industry would likely enable redistribution to a great extent (pp. 5–7 [355]).

        1.3.4.4.1. Pro:
        If sex work were legalised, enforcement mechanisms, such as the auditing of sex workers, would ensure the accurate accounting [356] and collection of taxes.

        1.3.4.4.2. Con:
        There is precedent for the taxation of otherwise illegal goods and services [357], such as Quebec's decision to hold an illegal male escort agency accountable for back taxes [358].

        1.3.4.4.3. Pro:
        It has been estimated that each legally licensed sex worker in the US could contribute over USD $5,000 in taxes per year (p. 9 [359]).

        1.3.4.4.4. Con:
        In Amsterdam, only 5% of sex workers [360] have registered to pay tax, making such earnings marginal at best.

  1.4. Pro:
  The government has no right to outlaw an interaction between consenting adults.

    1.4.1. Pro:
    The criminalisation of sex work is an infringement on sex workers' right to privacy, as it polices conduct that is typically private (p. 643 [361]).

      1.4.1.1. Pro:
      In the US, the criminalisation of sex work is at odds with the constitutional right to privacy [362].

        1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
        In Lawrence v. Texas [363], the US Supreme Court ruled that one's personal decisions about sex should be free from state interference.

      1.4.1.2. Pro:
      The criminalisation of sex work inherently involves an undue scrutiny of others' sex lives and has been linked with voyeuristic and exhibitionist tendencies in society (p. 12 [364]).

        1.4.1.2.1. Pro:
        In a 2022 ruling about the rights of sex workers, the Supreme Court of India noted how their privacy rights are often violated by voyeuristic media [365].

        1.4.1.2.2. Pro:
        Treating sex as a public, rather than private, matter is a method of ideological control [366] by social institutions.

        1.4.1.2.3. Con:
        Evidence has shown that a lack of government intervention in sex work has often resulted in worse conditions for workers (p. 11 [367]).

        1.4.1.2.4. Con:
        There are many ways in which private sexual activity is – and should be – subject to law [368]; sex work is not unique in this way.

      1.4.1.3. Con:
      Sex work, as a commercial transaction [369], is inherently public in nature.

        1.4.1.3.1. Pro:
        Sex workers often solicit and serve clients on the streets or in other public venues (p. 202 [370]).

        1.4.1.3.2. Pro:
        Online sex workers [371] often use pop-up advertisements [372] to draw in and screen potential customers.

        1.4.1.3.3. Con:
        Commercial transactions can nonetheless be private [373] in nature; e.g., there is a reasonable expectation that self-funded therapy is not treated differently to publicly funded provisions.

        1.4.1.3.4. Con:
        The sex lives of individuals are not a matter of public interest [374] and should not be considered as such.

    1.4.2. Con:
    Governments should, and often do, outlaw interactions between consenting adults when they cause, incentivise, or carry significant risk of harm [375].

      1.4.2.1. Con:
      It is not the purpose of the state to protect people from the consequences of their own free choices (pp. 255, 258 [376]).

        1.4.2.1.1. Con:
        One of the primary reasons for the formation of governments is protecting people from themselves [377] by incentivising safe and rational behaviour.

        1.4.2.1.2. Pro:
        If an individual's choices hurt no one [378], they should not be obstructed. Since sex work is a victimless crime [379], it should be legalised.

      1.4.2.2. Pro:
      In many cases, activities that cause physical harm are considered assault despite the consent of the involved parties (p. 37 [380]).

        1.4.2.2.1. Con:
        Many athletes risk serious injury, including long-term brain damage, to play at a professional level (p. 2 [381]). Thus, government concern about physical harm is highly context dependent.

        1.4.2.2.2. Con:
        Such acts are illegal because the harm they cause is so great that one cannot reasonably consent [382] to them. This is not the case with sex work.

          1.4.2.2.2.1. Pro:
          Sex work does not inherently result in physical harm.

        1.4.2.2.3. Pro:
        Despite the strict consent norms around BDSM (p. 172 [383]), many jurisdictions [384] do not allow practitioners to legally consent to harm within a sexual context.

          1.4.2.2.3.1. Pro:
          Consensual BDSM practices have been prosecuted [385] as criminal assaults on many occassions.

        1.4.2.2.4. Pro:
        Though both organ donation and financial exchange are legal, it is nonetheless illegal to sell one's organs [386]. An individual can be prosecuted [387] for facilitating such sales, even if a donor has consented to the exchange.

        1.4.2.2.5. Pro:
        Self-harming behavior is considered sufficient legal justification for depriving a person of their right to liberty (p. 5 [388]).

    1.4.3. Pro:
    The right to bodily autonomy [389] is meaningless unless it extends to the right to use one's body for acts that others may not agree with or support.

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      Sex work allows the bodies of workers to be used and controlled by others, which severely diminishes their bodily autonomy.

        1.4.3.1.1. Con:
        The legalisation of sex work would cement sex work as a financial exchange, guaranteeing the bodily autonomy of women to a greater extent.

          1.4.3.1.1.1. Con:
          Sex workers in Britain make around GBP £20,000 [390] per year, well below the national average wage [391]. Thus, the financial autonomy gained from sex work is minimal compared to other jobs.

          1.4.3.1.1.2. Pro:
          Sex workers in the UK work only around 10 hours per week [392] and typically report high job satisfaction, showing that the industry can support workers' bodily and financial autonomy.

          1.4.3.1.1.3. Con:
          Women's self-ownership should not depend on financial empowerment because, in a world where men control most wealth [393], their autonomy would remain reliant on men's interests.

        1.4.3.1.2. Con:
        In consensual sex work, workers are able to negotiate and choose the ways in which their body is or is not used, thereby protecting their autonomy (p. 85 [394]).

        1.4.3.1.3. Pro:
        Sex work grants a client an unreasonable amount of control over the body of the worker, which treats them as property (p. 22 [395]).

      1.4.3.2. Con:
      The right to bodily autonomy does not allow [396] individuals to inflict harm upon others or society writ large.

        1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
        For example, governments often make some vaccinations mandatory [397] for citizens, prioritizing public health over bodily autonomy.

        1.4.3.2.2. Con:
        The alleged harm of consensual sex work is moral in nature. Since moral values are relative [398], the state should not intervene to enforce them and should instead focus on material harms.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      Criminalising sex work denies sex workers and clients the ability to choose with whom and under what conditions they have sex [399]; this choice should not be subject to the approval of the state.

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
        Restrictions on how individuals can use their bodies obstruct their ability to express and define their personalities [400], as bodies are the means through which humans interact with the world.

        1.4.3.3.2. Pro:
        If it is acceptable to choose with whom one wants to have non-commercial sex, then it should also be acceptable to do so when the sex is commercial, as financial compensation is merely another factor to consider when picking a sexual partner.

          1.4.3.3.2.1. Pro:
          Within non-commercial sexual relationships, there remains a direct correlation between financial consumption and mating success (p. 603 [401]).

          1.4.3.3.2.2. Con:
          Considering the financial situation of a potential sexual partner amongst other factors is different from predicating the entire interaction on financial compensation since the latter does not represent the type of healthy, mutual investment [402] found in stable, rewarding relationships.

        1.4.3.3.3. Con:
        The state has compelling interests [403] in ensuring the health and rights of its citizens and combating crime, which justify its interference in the case of sex work.

      1.4.3.4. Pro:
      In the US, the right to bodily autonomy extends to a number of potentially harmful acts, such as refusing recommended medical treatments [404].

        1.4.3.4.1. Con:
        Bodily autonomy does not protect a number of acts that are likely to result in harm to the individual or society at large, such as the use of illegal drugs (p. 17 [405]).

        1.4.3.4.2. Pro:
        The US Supreme Court has repeatedly affirmed bodily integrity as a sphere in which the government may not reasonably intrude (p. 19 [406]).

    1.4.4. Con:
    When one person is only having sex to get paid, the sex is not fully consensual, and so it should not be allowed or encouraged.

      1.4.4.1. Con:
      The sex industry is often a means of providing for oneself or a family when all other options have been exhausted [407]. It is better to have this avenue than to be entirely powerless.

        1.4.4.1.1. Pro:
        Many people do jobs that they find less than satisfying [408] to secure resources for themselves and their families. In a capitalist society, this is not generally considered non-consensual.

        1.4.4.1.2. Pro:
        It would be more helpful for the government to address the underlying economic and social circumstances – such as widespread inequality and poverty – that make sex work the best available choice than to ban sex work altogether (pp. 4, 6 [409]).

        1.4.4.1.3. Con:
        People in financially dire straits would be better supported through interventions that helped them access other careers (p. 44 [410]). The legalisation of sex work would leave them stuck in a career otherwise considered a last resort.

        1.4.4.1.4. Con:
        Sex workers are often mistakenly presented as a homogeneous group; many sex workers are neither impoverished nor desperate [411].

      1.4.4.2. Pro:
      Although many adults consent to sex work, this does not always constitute enthusiastic consent [412], where participants actively communicate and reaffirm their desire.

        1.4.4.2.1. Pro:
        Banning abusive transactions, even those that are seemingly consensual, takes power from the abusers and protects the victims. This is a common practice [413] in most countries.

          1.4.4.2.1.1. Con:
          Banning all sex work transactions just because some are abusive is excessive. It makes more sense to find ways to prevent [414] the abusive ones.

          1.4.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          Most developed countries set a minimum wage [415] to limit the extent of the exploitation to which employees can be exposed, making abusive business practices less common.

          1.4.4.2.1.3. Con:
          A person's financial situation, however bad it may be, is not the fault of another person in a transaction. To imply that consent is lost due to financial hardship assigns undue blame.

          1.4.4.2.1.4. Pro:
          A junior may consent to sex with a senior in exchange for a raise or promotion, yet such quid pro quo sexual exchanges [416] are illegal.

        1.4.4.2.2. Con:
        The consent given by sex workers is better thought of as conditional [417]. They consent to perform a service provided certain stipulations are adhered to, and their consent is revoked if not.

        1.4.4.2.3. Con:
        Most countries use affirmative consent [418] rather than enthusiastic consent [419] as their legal model.

          1.4.4.2.3.1. Pro:
          Enthusiastic consent does not cohere with the realities of sex for both sex workers and other people, e.g., asexuals [420]. It is entirely feasible to consent to sex without being overjoyed about the act.

          1.4.4.2.3.2. Con:
          Regardless of the minimum legal requirements for establishing consent, society should actively promote the highest standard of consent [421], which is impossible in sex work.

        1.4.4.2.4. Pro:
        Sex workers often consent to sex for mundane purposes that are disassociated from the norms of human desire; over time, this undermines their sense of autonomy [422].

      1.4.4.3. Pro:
      Monetary exchange creates an inequitable power dynamic that undermines free choice (p. 10 [423]). As such, sex workers cannot freely consent to sex acts.

        1.4.4.3.1. Pro:
        Governments often protect individuals from harmful consensual financial transactions. For example, online platforms have a legal duty to protect their users from fraudulent advertising practices [424].

        1.4.4.3.2. Con:
        Uneven power dynamics are often a feature of consensual adult sex (pp. 1–2 [425]); this is not inherently wrong and does not call for criminalisation.

        1.4.4.3.3. Pro:
        The threat of having no source of income can pressure one into agreeing to sex work, even if it causes them misery.

        1.4.4.3.4. Con:
        When individuals voluntarily choose to participate in a financial exchange, they are opting to participate in the associated power dynamics (p. 17 [426]).

      1.4.4.4. Con:
      An ulterior motive for having sex apart from innate desire is not enough to make that sex non-consensual.

        1.4.4.4.1. Pro:
        Consent is defined [427] as voluntary agreement. Therefore, if one voluntarily agrees to have sex for money, it is fully consensual, regardless of the conditionality of such an action.

        1.4.4.4.2. Con:
        Consent is valid only when not consenting to an act is an equally non-threatening option. In situations where one must engage in sex work for survival, such acts are coerced by their circumstances (p. 784 [428]).

          1.4.4.4.2.1. Con:
          Regardless of the circumstances, preventing an individual who is dependent on sex work for survival from engaging in it would essentially condemn them to worse suffering, to which they also cannot reasonably consent [429].

          1.4.4.4.2.2. Pro:
          Survival sex work is common amongst homeless women and youth, who may not have other options [430] (p. 2 [431]).

    1.4.5. Pro:
    Consensual sex involving financial exchange is legal in other contexts.

      1.4.5.1. Pro:
      Pornography involves both monetary compensation and sexual activity and is legal in many jurisdictions where sex work remains illegal.

        1.4.5.1.1. Pro:
        Pornography is legal [432] and widespread [433] in the US, but sex work remains illegal [434] in most areas.

        1.4.5.1.2. Con:
        Pornography performers are not paid for sex [435]; they are paid for acting in roles that may require them to engage in sex.

          1.4.5.1.2.1. Con:
