          There is no way of reliably determining the intention [436] of someone producing pornography; it is possible that their intention is to pay for sex, only using the film as a legal shield.

          1.4.5.1.2.2. Pro:
          Pornography is generally not spontaneous sex [437] but is scripted and directed to meet a producer's specifications.

        1.4.5.1.3. Pro:
        Sex workers and pornography performers have identical financial motivations for participating in sex (p. 245 [438]).

          1.4.5.1.3.1. Con:
          It is in society's interest to enable male sexual satiation (p. 329 [439]); while both sex work and pornography do so, sex work exposes clients to a range of risks, while pornography does not (p. 248 [440]).

      1.4.5.2. Pro:
      Marriage often involves both consensual sex and financial exchange, yet it is legal while sex work is not.

        1.4.5.2.1. Pro:
        Many marriages are motivated by the desire for increased wealth and security [441]; people often seek to marry a partner with better financial means.

        1.4.5.2.2. Con:
        Sex between married partners is not predicated on financial exchange. Transactional and conditional sex damages the intimacy [442] between married couples.

          1.4.5.2.2.1. Con:
          In some societies, it is common practice to refrain from consummating a marriage until a dowry has been paid (p. 2 [443]).

      1.4.5.3. Con:
      The context [444] in which sex work occurs is a primary motivation for its illegality.

        1.4.5.3.1. Con:
        The issues presented by the context of sex work are results of, rather than reasons for, its illegality [445].

          1.4.5.3.1.1. Con:
          Sexual relationships are inherently private in nature, so even in a legalized context, sex work inherently creates opportunities for abuse and exploitation (p. 224 [446]).

          1.4.5.3.1.2. Pro:
          When their services are criminalised, sex workers often work in isolated, outdoor spaces to avoid police intervention. This makes them comparatively more [447] vulnerable to sexual assault (pp. 524–525 [448]).

        1.4.5.3.2. Pro:
        In a society characterised by pervasive male violence [449], sex work cannot feasibly be made safer.

          1.4.5.3.2.1. Con:
          Even if legalisation cannot improve the safety of sex work, it might reduce overall male violence by acting as a replacement for sexual assault (p. 6 [450]).

          1.4.5.3.2.2. Pro:
          Sex work unavoidably imbues men with more opportunities to abuse and exploit women [451] as they have financial power over them.

      1.4.5.4. Con:
      Since each context involves a number of unique considerations, it is impossible to equate the legal status of sex work with that of other, distinct contexts.

  1.5. Con:
  Criminalisation is necessary to decrease the prevalence of sex work and reduce its inherent harms [452].

    1.5.1. Pro:
    Sex work harms the psychological health of sex workers (p. 16 [453]). This would likely continue despite legalisation.

      1.5.1.1. Con:
      Removing the fear of arrest or prosecution is likely to significantly reduce sex workers' anxiety (pp. 946–947 [454]).

        1.5.1.1.1. Pro:
        When sex work is illegal, the records associated with criminal conviction [455] can make it impossible for workers to transition to most other careers.

        1.5.1.1.2. Con:
        Other factors, including drug use, minority gender identity, and childhood trauma, are more strongly associated with the mental health of sex workers than legal concerns (p. 7 [456]).

      1.5.1.2. Con:
      A greater ability to discuss and be honest about their work is likely to help sex workers build more effective support networks and better relationships, reducing their likelihood of depression (p. 5 [457]).

        1.5.1.2.1. Pro:
        Much of the trauma [458] related to sexual issues stems from shaming. The normalisation of sex work that would occur with legalisation would reduce the shame and stigma experienced by sex workers.

        1.5.1.2.2. Con:
        Studies have demonstrated that sex work affects the personal romantic relationships of sex workers in predominantly negative ways (p. 1 [459]).

          1.5.1.2.2.1. Pro:
          Sex workers often experience difficulties in their relationships outside of work due to issues of trust, guilt, and jealousy (p. 3 [460]). This is unlikely to be addressed by legalisation.

          1.5.1.2.2.2. Pro:
          The stigmatisation of their work can prevent sex workers from accessing the personal and financial benefits of marriage [461].

          1.5.1.2.2.3. Con:
          Nobody needs to disclose that they are a current or former sex worker, so it is unlikely that this stigma affects relationships.

      1.5.1.3. Pro:
      Sex work often requires exhausting and potentially harmful emotional labour [462] to satisfy clients; psychological ill-health is likely to always be part of the profession.

        1.5.1.3.1. Pro:
        Performing emotional labour can lead to stress-related disorders, which can contribute to cumulative harm (p. 1 [463]).

        1.5.1.3.2. Con:
        Since emotional labour is an essential part [464] of many service jobs, it should not be used as an argument against legal sex work.

      1.5.1.4. Pro:
      Many legal sex workers report feeling isolated or excluded from their friends and families due to their profession (p. 4 [465]).

        1.5.1.4.1. Pro:
        In Zurich, where sex work is legal and regulated, sex workers report higher rates of depression and anxiety than the general population (p. 1 [466]).

          1.5.1.4.1.1. Con:
          This study does not include information about the mental health of sex workers prior to legalisation, which makes it difficult to assess how the change has impacted psychological health.

          1.5.1.4.1.2. Con:
          An association between sex work and high rates of depression and anxiety does not demonstrate causation [467].

          1.5.1.4.1.3. Pro:
          Many of the respondents reported that their mental health was damaged due to financial insecurity, worry of infection, issues in their intimate relationships, and feelings of guilt, among other issues (pp. 4–5 [468]).

        1.5.1.4.2. Con:
        Illegal sex workers are often isolated [469] from essential services for fear of reputational damage, harming their physical and psychological health.

        1.5.1.4.3. Pro:
        A 2007 Dutch government report found that sex workers had increased their use of sedatives [470] in the face of poorer emotional well-being despite legalisation.

        1.5.1.4.4. Con:
        Sex work is not unusually particular in this regard; numerous legal professions report a high degree of loneliness and isolation [471].

    1.5.2. Con:
    It is impossible to eradicate sex work, so governments should aim to mitigate the associated harms through legalisation.

      1.5.2.1. Con:
      It is impossible to eradicate [472] murder. However, legalising murder would be immoral, unpopular, and would likely lead to more deaths.

        1.5.2.1.1. Con:
        As it inherently involves the nonconsensual death of a human being, murder [473] is harmful in a way that sex work is not; a comparison of the two is not especially useful.

          1.5.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          While murder is not legal, some regulated forms of assisted death are permissible [474]. As with sex work, the hope is that legalisation will make an inevitable practice safer.

        1.5.2.1.2. Pro:
        The decision whether to commit murder often involves a consideration of the possible penalty (p. 54 [475]); in the absence of any repercussions, murder would likely increase dramatically.

      1.5.2.2. Con:
      The failure of prior efforts to eradicate sex work does not mean that it is impossible to do so.

        1.5.2.2.1. Pro:
        Universal basic income could eradicate poverty, removing a main motivation for people to engage in sex work (p. 494 [476]).

        1.5.2.2.2. Con:
        While it might be possible to achieve the eradication of sex work in a post-work economy [477], the current state of society precludes [478] this from occurring.

        1.5.2.2.3. Pro:
        Prior efforts to eradicate sex work may have been insufficient in numerous correctable ways, such as a lack of enforcement or having punishments that were not severe enough.

      1.5.2.3. Pro:
      Sex work is a natural consequence of sexuality.

        1.5.2.3.1. Pro:
        The characteristics of human sexuality and procreation have led men and women to develop different reproductive strategies (p. 2 [479]); this creates the marketplace for monetized sexual activity.

          1.5.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          Some sociologists theorise that men desire sex at twice the rate of women, fuelling demand for paid sexual services (p. 6 [480]).

        1.5.2.3.2. Con:
        Human sexual behaviour is not solely driven by human nature; humans have the ability to create social norms surrounding sex based on other concerns (p. 6 [481]).

        1.5.2.3.3. Pro:
        Forms of sex work have even been observed throughout the animal kingdom (p. 526 [482]).

      1.5.2.4. Pro:
      Maintaining laws that contradict common social practices can encourage disrespect for law enforcement and undermine the rule of law [483] more generally.

        1.5.2.4.1. Pro:
        When laws are regularly broken [484], the legitimacy of the legislative process creating them is called into question, as seen in other domains [485].

        1.5.2.4.2. Pro:
        Unequal enforcement of laws undermines the notion of a just society [486] and reinforces class division.

        1.5.2.4.3. Con:
        The legality of sex work has little bearing on broader perceptions of law; sex work is a distinct, legally problematic area (p. 141 [487]).

        1.5.2.4.4. Con:
        Law is based upon public policies that are determined by legislators and upheld by the use of force rather than on social norms (p. 6 [488]).

    1.5.3. Con:
    Legalising sex work would reduce existing stigmas surrounding sex and sexuality, which are a source of many of the harms typically associated with sex work.

      1.5.3.1. Pro:
      Reducing the stigma around sexuality would make governments more likely to create policies on sexual matters based on evidence [489] rather than social norms (pp. 1–2 [490], p. 1 [491]).

        1.5.3.1.1. Con:
        Laws represent the codification of social norms [492]; as such, they should be based upon subjective community consensus rather than objective evidence.

        1.5.3.1.2. Pro:
        Many government policies, such as those surrounding divorce [493], were previously seen as issues of religious morality. Stigma has lessened as policy has become increasingly secular [494].

      1.5.3.2. Con:
      Sex work legalisation would only reduce the stigma surrounding transactional sex (p. 204 [495]). More widespread norms of sex and sexuality would likely be unaffected.

        1.5.3.2.1. Con:
        Any particular sexual practice, even if transactional, is difficult to separate from the wider norms surrounding sexual expectations and desires (p. 1 [496]).

        1.5.3.2.2. Pro:
        Given that most people do not use [497] sex work services, new norms created through the legalisation of sex work are unlikely to spread to society more generally.

        1.5.3.2.3. Pro:
        Mainstream social sexual norms are typically formed in the arts and media. Viewpoints crafted in marginalised spheres, like those regarding transactional sex, are unlikely to be influential (p. 211 [498]).

      1.5.3.3. Pro:
      Legalisation would allow sex workers to take control of the sociopolitical discourse surrounding sex work since there would be less danger in openly talking about their experiences.

        1.5.3.3.1. Con:
        Governments are likely to continue ignoring sex workers after legalisation because they represent a politically marginalised part of society.

          1.5.3.3.1.1. Con:
          Candidates of the Australian Sex Party [499] – a sex-industry lobby group [500] now included in the Reason Party [501] – have been elected [502] to state legislatures.

          1.5.3.3.1.2. Pro:
          The success of interest groups depends not on their size but on the willingness of mediating institutions and politicians to support them (p. 70 [503]).

        1.5.3.3.2. Con:
        Given their relative social and political isolation [504], sex workers are unlikely to be a major force in society even if their profession were legal, so the discourse regarding sex work may not change.

          1.5.3.3.2.1. Con:
          There are over 1 million sex workers [505] in the US alone; sex worker unions could be as large as the powerful unions made up of auto workers [506] and food and commercial workers [507].

          1.5.3.3.2.2. Pro:
          Despite having legalised sex work, German discourse is replete with stigmatising depictions of sex work intended to elicit disgust (p. 325 [508]).

          1.5.3.3.2.3. Pro:
          Despite the decriminalisation of sex work in 2003, stigmatising media discourses about New Zealand sex workers continue to proliferate (p. 40 [509]).

        1.5.3.3.3. Pro:
        In the Netherlands, Karina Schaapman used her new-found political influence to publicise a book about her experiences as a sex worker (p. 237 [510]).

        1.5.3.3.4. Pro:
        Some sex workers use their platforms to create educational experiences that challenge [511] stereotypes and discrimination within the industry.

      1.5.3.4. Con:
      -> See 1.1.5.1.3.2.

    1.5.4. Con:
    Sex workers provide an important and valuable service that more people could access if it were legalised.

      1.5.4.1. Pro:
      Sex work allows people to have more varieties of sex than they could easily have in the context of a casual hookup or relationship.

        1.5.4.1.1. Pro:
        Many people have strong preferences for particular sexual fetishes or acts that most other people do not enjoy. Sex work allows them to pay for this form of sexual fulfilment.

          1.5.4.1.1.1. Pro:
          People often feel embarrassed by their unconventional sexual desires. Sex work allows them to fulfil such desires in a more anonymous fashion than hookups or relationships.

          1.5.4.1.1.2. Pro:
          In the context of a relationship or a hookup, one's partner may not be willing to entertain a specific sexual desire if it is unusual or they do not share it themselves.

          1.5.4.1.1.3. Con:
          Polyamorous, BDSM, or other fetish clubs often offer varied opportunities for those with less common desires, without requiring the legalisation of sex work.

        1.5.4.1.2. Con:
        Niche hookup apps [512] and dating services [513] cater to those with niche sexual desires.

        1.5.4.1.3. Pro:
        This can be particularly important for those in committed relationships with partners who are physically or psychologically unable to have sex, who may (with their partner's agreement) want to employ sex workers.

      1.5.4.2. Con:
      Sex work often takes place in inconvenient places [514] that can make the act less enjoyable and decrease the likelihood of people genuinely benefitting from the service.

        1.5.4.2.1. Pro:
        Visiting a brothel can be uncomfortable [515], especially when doing so for the first time.

        1.5.4.2.2. Con:
        Sex work often takes place in inconvenient places because, in an illegal system, it must be hidden from law enforcement. This would change with legalisation.

        1.5.4.2.3. Pro:
        In Zurich [516], where sex work is legal, special car boxes are supplied but are hardly comfortable.

      1.5.4.3. Con:
      The remorse or anxiety that some people feel after hiring a sex worker can reduce its value as a service (pp. 28, 58 [517]).

        1.5.4.3.1. Con:
        Such feelings may be based on the illegality of sex work and the negative social stigma attached to it rather than negative experiences with sex work itself (pp. 23, 49 [518]).

        1.5.4.3.2. Pro:
        Many men interviewed about their experiences of hiring sex workers have emphasised their concerns over participating in an exploitative industry (p. 28 [519]).

        1.5.4.3.3. Con:
        The study in the parent claim was performed by an anti-sex-work group, who may have interpreted the participants' responses in a particularly negative light due to their conflict of interest (p. 5 [520]).

        1.5.4.3.4. Pro:
        Given that social anxiety [521] is one of the primary motivations for hiring sex workers, the additional stress associated with sex work directly decreases its usefulness and value to clients.

      1.5.4.4. Pro:
      Sex work may be useful for people in open relationships who want to ensure they do not form emotional bonds with sexual partners outside of their primary [522] partnership.

        1.5.4.4.1. Pro:
        Open relationships are also often stigmatised [523] by society; sex workers can provide a valuable and understanding avenue for exploring alternative relationship styles.

        1.5.4.4.2. Con:
        Open relationships, and particularly those involving sex work, carry a number of emotional and physical risks [524].

      1.5.4.5. Pro:
      Legal sex work would help people who have difficulty finding sexual partners to have sex.

        1.5.4.5.1. Pro:
        The option to hire a sex worker can be particularly important for people who face social stigma, such as disabled people [525].

          1.5.4.5.1.1. Pro:
          Disabled [526] people are less likely to have a long-term partner or marry than non-disabled people. Thus, hiring a sex worker may offer them a valuable avenue for intimacy.

          1.5.4.5.1.2. Con:
          A number of prominent disabled activists [527] are uncomfortable with the idea of paying for sex, as it leaves unchanged the root causes of ableist attitudes to sexuality and desire.

          1.5.4.5.1.3. Pro:
          For disabled people, sex can affirm [528] their normalcy and place in society. Allowing this to be achieved more easily through the legal hire of sex workers would be highly valuable.

        1.5.4.5.2. Pro:
        Lonely people [529], such as older people [530] or deployed members of the military [531], often rely on sex work to find intimacy due to their isolation from others.

          1.5.4.5.2.1. Con:
          Sex work districts near military bases are heavily associated with the exploitation of workers [532] (p. 2 [533]); therefore, facilitating soldiers' needs may not be a net positive for society.

          1.5.4.5.2.2. Pro:
          The militaries of many nations have officially or unofficially relied on sex work [534] to provide companionship and physical intimacy to their soldiers.

        1.5.4.5.3. Con:
        Governments should work to improve community support of such people [535] instead of relying on sex work as a stopgap to remediate loneliness.

        1.5.4.5.4. Con:
        If people who struggle to find sexual partners hire sex workers instead, they may stop seeking meaningful relationships (pp. 36–37 [536]); this can harm their emotional well-being [537] in the long term.

      1.5.4.6. Con:
      Those with sex addiction [538] frequently participate in sex work as workers or clients. Even if sex work does not cause this addiction, it allows for it to be worsened.

        1.5.4.6.1. Con:
        Even if sex work contributes to addiction, this would not necessarily imply that it should be illegal. For example, alcohol and coffee are addictive but are legal in most countries.

        1.5.4.6.2. Con:
        The existence of sex addiction has been widely debunked [539] by modern psychology.

        1.5.4.6.3. Pro:
        Some estimate that the destructive effects of sex addiction affect 60% or more of the male population [540].

    1.5.5. Pro:
    Sex work is systemically exploitative (p. 207 [541], pp. 180–181 [542]).

      1.5.5.1. Pro:
      Exploitation typically refers to transactions where one leverages the vulnerability [543] of another person to get them to commit an action they might not otherwise do. As such, even if sex workers consent, they are still being exploited.

        1.5.5.1.1. Con:
        Vulnerable people often take on jobs [544] that hurt or exploit them without the government intervening. Thus, it is illegitimate for the state to uniquely intervene in the case of sex work.

        1.5.5.1.2. Pro:
        Economic vulnerability is a primary motivation for performing sex work (pp. 82, 88 [545]).

      1.5.5.2. Con:
      Many sex workers find their work enjoyable [546] or rewarding; voluntarily performing a job that one enjoys does not meet the criteria for exploitation [547].

        1.5.5.2.1. Pro:
        A majority of surveyed female escorts and clinic sex workers in Denmark cited sexual curiosity and pre-existing interest in sex [548] as their motivation for entering sex work.

        1.5.5.2.2. Pro:
        Some sex workers particularly enjoy empathising [549] with others and helping them to achieve sexual empowerment.

        1.5.5.2.3. Con:
        Sex workers, in both legal and illegal settings, often wish to leave their work (p. 17 [550]). This undermines the idea that sex work is particularly enjoyable or empowering.

          1.5.5.2.3.1. Con:
          Sex workers in Australia, where sex work is legal (p. 99 [551]), have higher job satisfaction than those in countries where sex work is illegal (p. 11 [552]).

          1.5.5.2.3.2. Pro:
          A rescue industry [553] has formed around the idea that sex workers need to be saved.

          1.5.5.2.3.3. Con:
          There is a high rate of turnover intention in numerous industries [554]; such a trend does not justify making an entire industry illegal.

          1.5.5.2.3.4. Pro:
          The majority of sex workers cannot leave the industry due to external systemic factors, such as a lack of job training [555], asylum [556], or healthcare [557].

        1.5.5.2.4. Con:
        Even people who enjoy having sex may find sex work degrading [558], especially if they are treated poorly by clients [559].

      1.5.5.3. Con:
      Sex work NGOs often [560] lobby [561] in favour of legalisation. As they represent [562] the industry – and do valuable work informing the public – their interests should be recognised.

        1.5.5.3.1. Pro:
        The All India Network of Sex Workers [563] argues that ending India’s de facto criminalisation of sex workers would make them less vulnerable.

        1.5.5.3.2. Con:
        Some NGOs run by former sex workers, such as Apne Aap [564] and Space International [565], oppose legalisation, demonstrating that the sector does not unanimously support legalisation.

      1.5.5.4. Con:
      The freedom to choose whether or not to participate in sex work is a form of empowerment (p. 10 [566]).

        1.5.5.4.1. Pro:
        Sex work allows women to use seduction as a means of successfully operating within the existing confines of their society (p. 49 [567]).

        1.5.5.4.2. Con:
        In many communities, performing sex work is considered the epitome of disempowerment [568].

        1.5.5.4.3. Con:
        Viewing sex work as a form of empowerment is predominantly a first-world perspective that ignores the realities of sex workers in developing countries (pp. 1440–1441 [569]).

        1.5.5.4.4. Pro:
        Many sex workers have a high degree of autonomy, reflected by their ability to reject clients (p. 4 [570]).

          1.5.5.4.4.1. Pro:
          If sex work were legalised, more sex workers would be able to choose whether to accept individual customers [571], increasing the extent to which they are empowered.

          1.5.5.4.4.2. Con:
          Only high-class sex workers have a significant degree of choice over their clients. Typical sex workers do not have this freedom (p. 9 [572]).

      1.5.5.5. Pro:
      When recruiting sex workers, many pimps use courtship tactics to exploit a person's love (p. 31 [573]). Workers are more willing to perform sex work after having been brought into emotional dependency.

        1.5.5.5.1. Pro:
        The majority [574] of sex workers are exploitatively controlled by pimps (pp. 45–46 [575]).

        1.5.5.5.2. Con:
        The conditions under which sex workers are recruited is likely to improve when legalised or decriminalised, as the legitimacy of legal work can reduce predatory recruitment practices [576].

    1.5.6. Pro:
    Legalisation is likely to enhance both the demand and supply of sex work, increasing the number of people who experience the harms necessarily associated with the industry.

      1.5.6.1. Con:
      Legalisation would ameliorate a number of harms that are direct results of criminalisation itself rather than inherent effects of sex work.

        1.5.6.1.1. Pro:
        Many of the harms commonly associated with sex work are unnecessary results of its criminal status (p. 815 [577]).

        1.5.6.1.2. Con:
        There is evidence that the size of the illegal sex market, with its attendant harms, often expands after legalisation (p. 132 [578]).

      1.5.6.2. Con:
      Any increase in the harmful aspects of the sex work industry would depend on the type of control implemented [579] rather than legalisation itself.

        1.5.6.2.1. Con:
        By increasing the size of the sex industry, legalisation would automatically expand its harmful aspects (p. 4 [580]).

        1.5.6.2.2. Pro:
        Some forms of legalisation, such as that implemented in Nevada, grant pimps significant power [581] over individual sex workers.

      1.5.6.3. Pro:
      If legalised, the price of sex work would likely fall (p. 3 [582]); the availability of cheaper services [583] would likely increase the number of people hiring sex workers and the frequency with which they do so.

        1.5.6.3.1. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.4.1.1.

        1.5.6.3.2. Con:
        Legal sex workers would have to pay higher taxes [584] if their incomes were no longer hidden. The cost of their services would likely increase to absorb this expense.

          1.5.6.3.2.1. Con:
          Sex workers often file and pay taxes [585] regardless of the legality of their work.

          1.5.6.3.2.2. Pro:
          Given the range in attitudes and moral beliefs regarding sex work in many countries, legal sex work would likely be saddled with a sin tax [586], which can be a significant additional cost.

        1.5.6.3.3. Pro:
        Illegal black market goods and services like sex work are generally associated with a significant price mark-up, with some goods reaching a mark-up of 400% [587] or more (p. 25 [588]).

        1.5.6.3.4. Con:
        When sex work is illegal, the loss of all income due to imprisonment is a greater risk to sex workers' quality of life than lower prices.

      1.5.6.4. Pro:
      Prospective clients would no longer be deterred from paying for sex work due to concerns about committing an illegal act, increasing the demand for sex work (p. 1 [589]).

        1.5.6.4.1. Pro:
        Many men seek out sex workers to fulfill fairly common desires [590], such as frustration in relationships. Since many people have these desires, the potential pool of sex work customers is large.

        1.5.6.4.2. Con:
        The evidence from countries where sex work has been legalised suggests that the demand for sex work may not increase significantly.

          1.5.6.4.2.1. Pro:
          Despite the legalisation of sex work, the proportion of men in the Netherlands who have paid for a sex worker remains comparable to countries where sex work is illegal (pp. 1–2 [591]).

          1.5.6.4.2.2. Con:
          Since Spain decriminalised sex work [592] in 1995, the proportion of men who buy sex has increased; 40% of Spanish men are estimated to have paid for sex.

          1.5.6.4.2.3. Con:
          The sex work industry in Germany massively increased in size [593] with legalisation.

          1.5.6.4.2.4. Pro:
          The percentage of men who have paid for sex in Australia (about 17% [594]), where sex work is legal, is not significantly higher than the percentage in countries like the US (16% [595]), where it is illegal.

  1.6. Pro:
  The legalisation of sex work would improve the health and security of sex workers.

    1.6.1. Con:
    Legalised sex work is still not a safe job [596] overall. Even if it brings other benefits, legalisation cannot meaningfully change this.

      1.6.1.1. Con:
      Since there are numerous legal jobs that are equally or more dangerous [597], the danger associated with sex work is not a sufficient reason for outlawing it.

        1.6.1.1.1. Pro:
        Police officers are often at risk of being shot while on duty, and they exhibit a markedly lower life expectancy than the general population (p. 5 [598]).

        1.6.1.1.2. Pro:
        Doctors and other health care providers are routinely exposed to health hazards, such as viruses and super bugs [599].

        1.6.1.1.3. Con:
        The dangers associated with many occupations are inherent to the work, whereas sex workers are 18 times more likely to be victims of murder, often at the hands of repeat offenders (p. 1 [600]).

      1.6.1.2. Pro:
      In a five-country study, a majority of surveyed sex workers did not believe that legalisation would make their lives safer (p. 9 [601]).

        1.6.1.2.1. Con:
        In illegal markets, all actors look to make maximum profits [602], regardless of the safety of workers or customers. In a legal environment, regulations can prevent this from happening.

        1.6.1.2.2. Pro:
        Reports from countries with legalised sex work have demonstrated that legalisation only promotes the appearance of safety, and that violence against sex workers continues unabated (pp. 137–138 [603]).

    1.6.2. Pro:
    The legalisation of sex work would lead to health regulations and the promotion of safe sexual practices, which would improve the health of sex workers and clients (pp. 2352–2353 [604]).

      1.6.2.1. Con:
      Health examinations are generally only mandatory for sex workers rather than for their clients. This mitigates the potential health benefits of legalisation.

        1.6.2.1.1. Pro:
        The burden to check the health of clients is often placed on sex workers. The Scarlet Alliance, for example, advises sex workers to examine their clients' penises for signs of disease (pp. 184–185 [605]).

        1.6.2.1.2. Pro:
        Mandatory sexual health screenings are often viewed by sex workers as invasive and inconvenient (p. 10 [606]). Thus, regulation may not produce meaningfully better health outcomes.

          1.6.2.1.2.1. Pro:
          Mandatory testing of sex workers in Australia has been shown to impede, rather than facilitate, STI education and prevention (p. 203 [607]).

        1.6.2.1.3. Con:
        Regulations could require those purchasing sex to give proof of a recent sexual health exam.

        1.6.2.1.4. Con:
        If sex workers use appropriate protection [608], then there would likely be little need for customers to undergo STI testing.

      1.6.2.2. Pro:
      The legalisation and decriminalisation of sex work is associated with increased condom use amongst sex workers and decreased STI rates (p. 7 [609], p. 1 [610]).

        1.6.2.2.1. Pro:
        Where sex work is illegal, police have used the possession of condoms as evidence [611] in charges of sex work (p. 23 [612]), which deters [613] sex workers from using them and drives up STI rates (p. 16 [614]).

        1.6.2.2.2. Con:
        It is often difficult for sex workers to enforce condom use (p. 685 [615]). Thus, even if sex work were legalised, STI transmission rates may remain high.

          1.6.2.2.2.1. Pro:
          In many countries, there are taboos associated with discussing sexual matters and social norms about masculinity that make it difficult to enforce the use of condoms (p. 230 [616]).

        1.6.2.2.3. Pro:
        Legalisation often includes the strict enforcement of protected sex and enormous penalties on non-compliance. In Germany, sex workers who do not use condoms can be fined up to €50,000 (p. 2 [617]).

          1.6.2.2.3.1. Con:
          Clients often prefer unprotected sex (p. 1 [618]); this is reflected in the price premium that it attracts (p. 571 [619]).

        1.6.2.2.4. Con:
        Sex workers have strong incentives to use condoms regardless of whether sex work is legal.

          1.6.2.2.4.1. Con:
          Poorer sex workers are often not given adequate information regarding the need for condoms, leading many to undervalue their importance (pp. 1979–1980 [620]). Legalisation would make it easier to provide such workers with adequate education.

          1.6.2.2.4.2. Pro:
          Sex workers are at an increased risk of contracting an STI or becoming pregnant [621].

      1.6.2.3. Con:
      Governments would likely offer insufficient resources to support and protect sex workers after legalisation.

        1.6.2.3.1. Pro:
        If sex work were legal, governments would be less likely to invest in providing exit strategies for sex workers who want to leave the industry, as it would become no different [622] from any other profession.

          1.6.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          Governments have a compelling interest [623] in preventing citizens from performing criminal activities. Consequently, there is a greater urgency and need for governments to provide exit strategies when sex work is illegal.

          1.6.2.3.1.2. Con:
          Exiting an industry is more difficult when one must admit to participating in criminal activity to do so. Thus, illegal sex workers often must lie about or hide their previous employment when seeking to leave the industry (pp. 13, 54 [624]).

          1.6.2.3.1.3. Con:
          Governments currently provide little [625] in the way of exit strategies for sex workers. Non-profit organisations carry the vast burden of such work.

          1.6.2.3.1.4. Pro:
          When sex work is illegal, governments can justify funding exit programmes under the guise of protecting women (pp. 2, 17 [626]). Such justifications are no longer relevant when sex work is legal.

        1.6.2.3.2. Con:
        The legalisation of sex work would place pressure on governments to collaborate with sex workers to craft policy (p. 8 [627]). Without legalisation, such groups are often excluded from advising policymakers.

          1.6.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          Policymakers often craft industry-specific regulations in collaboration with representatives [628] of the industry. Sex workers would likely be involved in such discussions if sex work were legalised.

          1.6.2.3.2.2. Pro:
          In a legalised context, poor working conditions and crimes against sex workers would be considered failures of government, so politicians would be pushed [629] to make change.

          1.6.2.3.2.3. Con:
          Many legal forms of sex-related work, such as porn [630], face little oversight. There is little reason to believe sex work would be any different.

          1.6.2.3.2.4. Con:
          Many organisations claiming to advocate for sex workers actually represent the interests of pimps and the sex industry [631], so they may not truly be concerned with improving working conditions.

        1.6.2.3.3. Pro:
        Social safety nets and industry regulations [632] have been depleted all around the world, especially for informal workers [633]. Therefore, it is unlikely that the sex work industry will receive especially great amounts of focus or spending.

        1.6.2.3.4. Con:
        Sex worker support schemes in illegal contexts are often ineffective, as they do not reflect sex workers' needs, instead focusing on exiting the industry (p. 247 [634]). The reduction of funding to such services may be a positive effect of legalisation.

      1.6.2.4. Pro:
      As sex workers face a number of health risks, it is important that they can access medical advice and care, which is harder when they feel unsafe [635] telling their doctors about their work (p. 9 [636]).

        1.6.2.4.1. Con:
        Doctor-patient confidentiality [637] already ensures that sex workers can talk openly to their doctors. Improved social messaging could make this clearer to sex workers without necessitating legalisation.

          1.6.2.4.1.1. Con:
          In some cases, criminal activity [638] is considered a sufficient reason for breaking doctor-patient confidentiality, so sex workers risk being prosecuted if they seek healthcare.

        1.6.2.4.2. Pro:
        Decriminalising or legalising sex work can make policies to limit [639] the risks associated with drug use more effective because legal sex workers do not fear health care providers (p. 4 [640], p. 10 [641]).

    1.6.3. Pro:
    The legalisation of sex work could reduce instances of sexual assault (p. 3 [642]).

      1.6.3.1. Pro:
      Legal sex workers would be able to more readily share information about abusive clients to protect each other.

        1.6.3.1.1. Con:
        Regardless of legality, the prior verification of clients is impractical for many forms of sex work, e.g., street-based solicitation.

        1.6.3.1.2. Con:
        Regardless of whether or not sex work is legal, many sex workers are able to share information on abusive clients.

          1.6.3.1.2.1. Pro:
          Escorts often verify clients beforehand through verification agencies [643], even where their work is illegal.

          1.6.3.1.2.2. Pro:
          In England, the National Ugly Mugs database [644] has helped sex workers warn each other about dangerous clients and stores the profiles of such clients.

          1.6.3.1.2.3. Con:
          Online co-ordination between sex workers is often criminalised [645], making it much harder to ensure that they can work safely.

        1.6.3.1.3. Pro:
        Legal sex work businesses could perform criminal background checks on prospective clients [646] to identify those with a history of abuse.

      1.6.3.2. Con:
      Places with legalised sex work experience a greater incidence of sex crimes than areas without it (p. 2 [647]).

        1.6.3.2.1. Con:
        A cross-country study found no statistically significant relationship between violent crime, including rape, and the legalisation of sex work (p. 33 [648]).

          1.6.3.2.1.1. Con:
          Information from a wide range of countries, including the Netherlands [649], indicates that legal sex workers continue to consider petty physical assault a part of their job [650] (p. 305 [651]).

          1.6.3.2.1.2. Pro:
          Studies of European countries, such as the Netherlands [652] (p. 14 [653]), have found that the rate of sexual violence falls [654] when sex work is legalised.

        1.6.3.2.2. Con:
        Following Rhode Island's accidental legalisation [655] of indoor sex work, 31% fewer rapes were reported over two decades (p. 30 [656]).

          1.6.3.2.2.1. Con:
          Rhode Island only accidentally legalised indoor [657] sex work. Therefore, the results achieved there may not be duplicated were sex work to be more generally legalised (pp. 3, 26 [658]).

          1.6.3.2.2.2. Con:
          The overall crime rate in the US continued to decrease during the study period (p. 7 [659]). Thus, the reduced incidence of rape in Rhode Island might have been part of a larger phenomenon rather than an outcome of legalisation.

          1.6.3.2.2.3. Pro:
          Researchers [660] have suggested the decreased violence in Rhode Island was caused by the associated shift to indoor sex work, increased security spending in brothels, and the substitution of sex work for assault by violent male clients.

        1.6.3.2.3. Pro:
        Sex workers regularly face violent assaults in countries where sex work has been legalised or decriminalised (pp. 14, 17 [661]).

          1.6.3.2.3.1. Pro:
          Widespread abuse of sex workers in New Zealand has continued despite decriminalisation [662].

          1.6.3.2.3.2. Con:
          It is unrealistic to believe that legalisation will stop all abuse; instead, it will comparatively reduce [663] it.

          1.6.3.2.3.3. Con:
          Workers in a number of customer-facing roles frequently experience violence or harassment [664] on the job (p. 6 [665]). Assault is not an exclusive part of sex work, but rather a recurrent societal ill.

          1.6.3.2.3.4. Pro:
          The triggers that lead clients to assault or murder sex workers – such as money disputes or refusal to commit specific acts – are unlikely to be allayed by legalisation (p. 13 [666]).

        1.6.3.2.4. Pro:
        Legal sex work increases crime in wealthy countries (p. 39 [667]).

        1.6.3.2.5. Pro:
        There is evidence that those who buy sex more often may become more likely to commit sex crimes [668], such as committing rape. The legalisation of sex work would make it easier for such clients to purchase sex more often, thereby increasing the incidence of violent behaviours.

        1.6.3.2.6. Con:
        Brothel sex workers in Nevada, where sex work is legal, report low rates of violence (p. 287 [669]).

      1.6.3.3. Con:
      Sexual assault is caused by factors that are independent of sex work (p. 5 [670]). Thus, rates of sexual assault are unlikely to change significantly with the legalisation of sex work.

        1.6.3.3.1. Pro:
        Sexual violence is often linked to belief [671] in rigid gender norms and the legitimacy of violence. Such perspectives are unlikely to be changed by the legalisation of sex work.

        1.6.3.3.2. Pro:
        Perpetrators of sexual violence are more likely [672] to associate with violent or sexually aggressive peers. Such friendships are unaffected by the legal status of sex work.

        1.6.3.3.3. Con:
        Sexual violence is more likely [673] when perpetrators do not fear prosecution. Making it easier for sex workers to report crimes by decriminalising their work could, thus, make them safer.

    1.6.4. Pro:
    Legalisation would allow sex workers to more easily seek help from the police and courts if they are assaulted or robbed.

      1.6.4.1. Con:
      Legalisation makes it more difficult to prosecute the abusers [674] of sex workers because it removes the assumption that the transaction is inherently illegal or immoral.

        1.6.4.1.1. Pro:
        If sex work were legal, the burden of proof would be placed on victims to demonstrate that they have experienced harm or exploitation (p. 7 [675]).

          1.6.4.1.1.1. Con:
          This merely puts sex workers on the same footing as any other victim of sexual assault, which is the correct thing to do, given the presumption of innocence [676] in modern judicial systems.

          1.6.4.1.1.2. Pro:
          Rape cases have notoriously [677] low rates [678] of prosecution; without the criminalisation of sex work, sex workers may find themselves with little legal protection if they are sexually assaulted.

        1.6.4.1.2. Con:
        Criminalising otherwise positive activities to aid in the prosecution of abusive criminal activity is unethical (p. 1 [679]).

      1.6.4.2. Pro:
      A lack of legal recourse enables pimps to abuse and control sex workers, who are unlikely to go to the police [680] when sex work is a crime.

        1.6.4.2.1. Pro:
        In a legalised system, sex workers can report crimes to the authorities instead of their pimp. This reduces the burden on sex workers, as police intervention is more predictable.

        1.6.4.2.2. Con:
        The German police have found it more difficult to target abusive pimps [681] since sex work was legalised.

      1.6.4.3. Pro:
      When sex work is illegal, sex workers are particularly [682] vulnerable [683] to abuse [684] by law enforcement; thus, legalisation can reduce police corruption.

        1.6.4.3.1. Pro:
        Many sex workers have reported being raped, coerced into sex [685], and blackmailed [686] by police. Only if sex work were legalised could they report such assaults without fear [687] of reprisal.

        1.6.4.3.2. Con:
        Police corruption is often linked to institutional practices and departmental culture [688] (p. 2 [689]). As the legalisation of sex work would not change either of these factors, corruption is likely to endure.

      1.6.4.4. Pro:
      In court, sex workers have repeatedly been called unreliable witnesses [690] in their own cases or when testifying for others. Legalisation would help decrease such stigma.

        1.6.4.4.1. Con:
        A great [691] many people are deemed to be unreliable witnesses (p. 291 [692]). As this often is linked to poor memory recall [693], it is unlikely that legalisation would make sex workers seem more reliable.

        1.6.4.4.2. Con:
        Even if sex work were legal, lawyers would likely continue to play on existing stereotypes by painting sex worker testimony as unreliable [694] (pp. 17–18 [695]).

        1.6.4.4.3. Pro:
        Sex workers' poor relationship [696] with the police may lead them to be classed as hostile witnesses [697] or be depicted in court as unreliable [698].

      1.6.4.5. Con:
      Regardless of the legality of sex work, misogyny [699] is often rife in police departments, making it unlikely that sex workers will find justice for crimes against them.

        1.6.4.5.1. Pro:
        It is impossible for police departments to realistically address systemic misogyny, as policing is inherently coercive [700] in nature.

        1.6.4.5.2. Con:
        The criminalisation of sex work directly contributes to such misogyny by identifying sex workers as social undesirables [701] and removing the need to consider their perspectives.

    1.6.5. Con:
    The legalisation of sex work without the decriminalisation of the purchase of sex and associated businesses – often referred to as the Nordic Model [702] – is more likely to benefit sex workers.

      1.6.5.1. Pro:
      The Nordic Model [703] gives sex workers the ability to alert the police if pimps or customers break the law.

        1.6.5.1.1. Con:
        Under the Nordic Model, customers often wish [704] to remain anonymous, and sex workers are incentivised to help [705] them. Such actions, however, make sex workers less safe.

        1.6.5.1.2. Con:
        There is evidence that the criminalisation of clients leads to an increase in violence [706] against sex workers, making them more likely to need police assistance in the first place.

        1.6.5.1.3. Pro:
        Under the Nordic Model, the ability of sex workers to contact the police has deterred customers [707] – who are still breaking the law – from refusing to pay or acting pushy.

        1.6.5.1.4. Pro:
        Full decriminalisation would legally protect pimps, who often take advantage of sex workers [708].

      1.6.5.2. Con:
      Under the Nordic Model, sex workers often remain subject to other laws that reduce their quality of life [709].

        1.6.5.2.1. Pro:
        Under the Nordic Model, sex workers who work together are often treated as if they are one another's pimps and prosecuted [710].

        1.6.5.2.2. Pro:
        In Sweden, sex workers can lose custody of their children [711] and face eviction, as renting to a sex worker is considered procuring.

        1.6.5.2.3. Pro:
        Under the Nordic Model, foreign sex workers residing illegally in a country risk expulsion [712] if they report crimes to the police.

        1.6.5.2.4. Con:
        The existence of other problematic laws does not invalidate the potential benefits of the Nordic Model (pp. 8–9 [713]). Instead, it suggests that such laws should also be addressed.

        1.6.5.2.5. Con:
        In encouraging women to transition out [714] of sex work, the Nordic Model's primary goal is to increase their quality of life in the longer term.

          1.6.5.2.5.1. Pro:
          Countries that legalise sex work often allocate little funding [715] for exit services. Such funding is often a prime goal [716] of campaigns for the Nordic Model.

          1.6.5.2.5.2. Con:
          Since the Nordic Model still takes a negative view of sex workers, it often requires them to fulfill extremely difficult conditions [717] to access exit services, making them ineffective.

      1.6.5.3. Con:
      The criminalisation of demand for sex work undermines the occupational health of sex workers (p. 10 [718]).

        1.6.5.3.1. Pro:
        Criminalisation fails to account for the diversity of venues where sex work occurs, which hinders the development of appropriate occupational health strategies (pp. 208, 210 [719]).

        1.6.5.3.2. Con:
        The criminalisation of demand is not intended to promote sex work as a viable occupation, but to protect and divert sex workers while ultimately eradicating commercial sex [720].

      1.6.5.4. Pro:
      A number of countries consider the Nordic Model to be a successful method of addressing gender inequality, trafficking, and exploitation (p. 426 [721]).

        1.6.5.4.1. Con:
        The Nordic Model is opposed by many sex work and feminist advocacy groups [722].

          1.6.5.4.1.1. Con:
          There are a number of feminist organisations, such as the Coalition Against Trafficking in Women Australia (CATWA), that expressly support the Nordic Model [723].

          1.6.5.4.1.2. Pro:
          Many feminist groups that oppose the Nordic Model find it to be at odds with women's right to self-determination [724].

        1.6.5.4.2. Pro:
        Even opponents of the Nordic Model concur that it has been successful in reducing demand (p. 20 [725]).

        1.6.5.4.3. Pro:
        The number of men who have purchased sex has decreased from one in 8 to one in 13 [726] in Sweden, where the Nordic Model has been implemented.

        1.6.5.4.4. Con:
        It is logically inconsistent [727] to simultaneously consider sex work as something that is legal to sell but illegal to purchase (p. 821 [728]).

But the next time you hear that it is wrong to legislate morality, here are three things to remember. First, every law is a moral law. Speed limit laws are based on the idea that it’s wrong to endanger the lives of others. Recycling laws say it’s right to reuse things if you can because it’s wrong to abuse the environment. Arson laws say it’s wrong to burn down your neighbor’s house if his dog poops in your yard. From speed limits, to the criminal codes, to building regulations, to laws that govern business and protect consumers, each and every law asserts that some things are good and others are bad. Some thing are helpful and others are harmful. All laws are moral laws. Which leads to the second point. Since every law is a moral law, every law legislates morality."

 (1)There is no firm basis upon which to judge that theism or atheism is intrinsically more probable than the other. 

 (2)There is no firm basis upon which to judge that the total evidence favors theism or atheism over the other. 

It follows from (1) and (2) that

 (3)There is no firm basis upon which to judge that theism or atheism is more probable than the other. 

It follows from (3) that

 (4)Agnosticism is true: neither theism nor atheism is known to be true."

[125] https://fee.org/articles/is-labor-a-commodity/
"In this connection, it should be clear that the worth of every man’s service is similarly deter­mined, whether he be a strictly unskilled laborer or the most highly skilled artist, teacher, min­ister, butcher, baker, lawyer, en­gineer, business executive, or whatever. If he offers a service for sale, its value depends upon the highest bid acceptable to him in the free market.
[...]
The wage earner himself is no more a commodity than is the farmer whose labor results in a bag of potatoes. But the farmer should be free to sell either his labor or his potatoes; and so should every wage earner be free to offer his services as a commod­ity. Laborers or others who argue that labor is not a commodity would thus deny freedom of ex­change, which is the economic method—and the only one—that assures the laborer true and full value for his services."

Self-objectification is even found in women who view sexual objectification as harmless or even complimentary. Research has linked self-objectification to mental health outcomes such as depression, disordered eating, and reduced productivity. So, the sexual objectification of women indirectly contributes to their mental health problems because it leads to self-objectification."

-Decreased trust: If your partner keeps telling lies, it can have a direct impact on trust. The more lies they tell, the less you trust them or have faith in their honesty.

-Diminished compassion and empathy: Lying makes it harder to detect someone's emotions, which in turn, can diminish the compassion and empathy you feel toward that person.

-Lower intimacy: Intimacy requires emotional vulnerability, which can become nearly impossible without a foundation of trust and honesty.

-More lies and deception: One study found the brain can adapt to dishonesty. In other words, the more someone lies, the more their brain gets used to lying."

[...]
In addition to being important on their own, intimate threats can be precursors to more traditional forms of privacy and security threat. Intimate privacy invasions can result in the destruction of valuable or personal data, like financial records or family photographs. They can be the first step in financial fraud. In abusive partner situations, they can be a precursor to physical, emotional, and sexual abuse. And even well intentioned intimate monitoring can create a slippery slope of acceptability, inuring users to accepting surveillance as a mode of social control in other contexts."
[147] https://www.socialconnectedness.org/the-stigmatization-behind-sex-work/
"Four global legal systems regarding sex work exist. The first, full criminalization for all constituents participating in the sale of sex, induces stigmatization by deliberately branding sex workers as outlaws. The second – partial criminalization, which does not punish the buying and selling of sex, but all activities around it (e.g. brothels) – promotes a divide between society and sex workers, whom are constrained to work alone. The Nordic model (the third) aims at criminalizing buyers of sex. While in theory, this model aims at eliminating sex work, it only constrains sex workers into more secretive activities, further deepening the growing wedge of understanding with the rest of society. This overwhelmingly present stigma permeates the legal foundations of the fourth model, legalisation of sex work. States like Nevada segregate marginalized workers through the creation of special restrictions such as registration, strict health checks, and the purchase of a mandatory license."
[148] https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-030-64171-9_9

Although the industry is now decriminalised in New Zealand, the preliminary focus groups conducted in this research project found that there are still suggestions of continuing stigmatisation of sex workers, which impacts on the likelihood of disclosure of their occupation."

There are a variety of parts and forces at work, many of which resist disruptions of the status quo. All societies go through these types of changes at one point. You don’t need to be an avid student of history to know that. Consider a modern society and reflect on what it looked like hundreds of years ago. Often, society is unrecognizable."

[162] https://tryst.link/blog/sluts-for-security-phishing-101-tryst-sex-work-blog/
"Impersonation - one of the most common phishing attempts for sex workers is a scammer trying to get access to their online advertisements. They'll impersonate support teams, usually with an urgent request to fix an account issue or be removed from the site, with a link to their fake website that skims a password. Once they have access they'll change the deposit and contact details to theirs and collect deposits from clients straight to their bank account.

Spear phishing - unlike most phishing attempts that don’t aim at anyone in particular, sex workers are singled out as specific targets. The techniques used to phish generally can be made more specific and tailored to individual sex workers that are harder to detect than usual phishing techniques.

Unfortunately, as long as there's a degree of social shame and stigma around sex and sex work, these methods will be used to take advantage of innocent people and remain a soft target for scammers."

This means that, despite the criminals’ best efforts, someone who is skilled at investigating those records can solve these crimes. The owners of small brothels sometimes prefer to continue to run the brothel illegally rather than make the expensive changes that are required in order to secure a license."

2. A full set of fingerprints of the person, which shall be updated every six (6) years;

3. Complete employment record of the applicant for the preceding five (5) years;

4. All street and mailing address(es) of the person;
[...]
Investigation: The police shall investigate, through all available means, the accuracy of all information supplied by any applicant on the registration form."
[196] https://www.unodc.org/documents/human-trafficking/2008/BP020CorruptionandHumanTrafficking.pdf
"Corruption is one of the major contributing factors to the crime of trafficking in persons. It is both an underlying root cause and a facilitating tool to carry out and sustain this illicit trade. Worse corruption can also ensure safe havens for the profits collected as a result of human misery. Unfortunately, there has been very little exploration into the linkages of the two phenomena. When thinking of the images that join corruption and trafficking, one can easily imagine the border guard who is pocketing a few bills to not check a passport, or imagine the immigration officer who is willing to accept forged documents for a price, or the police offer who accepts a fee to turn a blind eye to a cry for help."

Foreign women from Asia and South America in commercial sex are at risk of sex trafficking, especially those who do not speak English and who work in private homes and informal or suburban environments, where they are more isolated from service providers. Some international students and temporary visa holders are at risk of sex and labor trafficking."

[221] https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=1986065
"This paper has investigated the impact of legalized prostitution on inflows of human trafficking. According to economic theory, there are two effects of unknown magnitude. The scale effect of legalizing prostitution leads to an expansion of the prostitution market and thus an increase in human trafficking, while the substitution effect reduces demand for trafficked prostitutes by favoring prostitutes who have legal residence in a country. Our quantitative empirical analysis for a cross-section of up to 150 countries shows that the scale effect dominates the substitution effect. On average, countries with legalized prostitution experience a larger degree of reported human trafficking inflows."
[222] https://openworks.wooster.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=8328&context=independentstudy
"We found that under complete legalization, there was a huge rise in the populations of both the active customers and trafficked prostitutes. The reduction of the level of risk involved in indulging in prostitution services increased the active customer population from about 51% under a system of illegal prostitution, to approximately 84% of the entire population under complete legalization. This steep increase in demand cause trafficked prostitutes to rise from a population of about 15%, under a system of illegal prostitution, to a population of about 45% under legalization.

This was attributed to the fact that traffickers and brothel owners faced a very low intensity of risk and a high margin of profit for employing trafficked workers through low labour costs due to the exploitation of trafficked victims through the debt-bondage system described in Chapter 2."

[228] https://www.theguardian.com/uk/2009/oct/20/trafficking-numbers-women-exaggerated?intcmp=239
"The sex trafficking story is a model of misinformation. It began to take shape in the mid 1990s, when the collapse of economies in the old Warsaw Pact countries saw the working flats of London flooded with young women from eastern Europe. Soon, there were rumours and media reports that attached a new word to these women. They had been "trafficked".

And, from the outset, that word was a problem. On a strict definition, eventually expressed in international law by the 2000 Palermo protocol, sex trafficking involves the use of force, fraud or coercion to transport an unwilling victim into sexual exploitation. This image of sex slavery soon provoked real public anxiety.

But a much looser definition, subsequently adopted by the UK's 2003 Sexual Offences Act, uses the word to describe the movement of all sex workers, including willing professionals who are simply travelling in search of a better income."
[229] https://www.nswp.org/sites/default/files/SW%20is%20Not%20Trafficking.pdf
"Anti-traffickers often ascribe victim status to immigrants despite the fact they have made conscious and rational decisions to cross borders with the knowledge that they will be selling sex. Migrant sex workers who are detained in ‘raid and rescue’ operations, despite informing the authorities that they are not victims of trafficking, are liable to arrest and prosecution on two counts – as illegal immigrants and illegal sex workers. Their choices are limited to claiming the status of either victim or criminal. In this framework there is no space for women who choose to do sex work."

If anything, we can look to the 14 years of the Trafficking in Persons Report as the evidentiary link that sex work and trafficking are not connected."

[260] https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4261233/pdf/S1474746414000426a.pdf
"My research found variations in modes of self-employment comparable to those in the broader economy. The position of independent sex workers was similar to that of self-employed lone traders in other labour market sectors. They set their own terms and conditions, determined the services they would offer, their working hours and rates of pay. As Rachel, an independent worker in a collective, commented, independent sex work was ‘essentially like running your own business’, with both the flexibility and the financial uncertainties associated with self-employed work generally. Brothel and agency workers, however, while having notional self-employed status, were at the same time in an employment relationship with a third party, to whom they handed over a proportion of their wages. Agencies hiring sex workers took on responsibilities such as marketing and client liaison, although workers were usually able to negotiate their rates, working hours and services."

"The arguments behind calls to enforce the CLS are not only morally unassailable but make good economic sense as well. In fact, a growing body of evidence highlights the economic benefits of these core labor standards:

-Better worker rights result in higher productivity growth, thus leading to faster economic growth.

-Improved worker rights also tend to result in a better distribution of income, both among workers and between workers and firms. In other words, better worker rights lead to larger overall output that gets more evenly distributed.

-As the benefits of faster growth are more evenly distributed, local demand is stronger and more stable, thus reducing the chance of a financial crisis."

"In October 2021, the Edinburgh Employment Appeal Tribunal (“EAT”) gave judgment in A v Burke and Hare EA-2020-SCO-000067-DT, in which it refused anonymity to a former stripper. The Appellant, called A within the judgment, was allowed to keep her name private solely in respect of the anonymity proceedings. This meant she could only continue with her claim against her former workplace, which was for £1,846.56 in holiday pay, if she was named in the claim and any substantive judgments as a former stripper. A consequently withdrew her claim due to the potential repercussions of being exposed as a former stripper. The presiding judge, Lord Summers, accepted this would be the outcome of the decision, but nonetheless refused anonymity."
[274] http://employmentblog.practicallaw.com/anonymity-a-right-to-privacy-in-the-employment-tribunal/
"Employment tribunals have a discretionary power to make an order preventing or restricting the public disclosure of any aspect of proceedings (rule 50(1), Employment Tribunal Rules of Procedure 2013). In making an order, the tribunal must give full weight to the Convention right to freedom of expression and the principle of open justice, namely that justice is administered by the courts in public and is therefore open to public scrutiny. Employment tribunals have become increasingly reluctant to order any restriction on the full details of any case being ventilated in public, including more limited measures such as the redaction of names of individuals peripheral or irrelevant to the issues."
[275] http://employmentblog.practicallaw.com/anonymity-a-right-to-privacy-in-the-employment-tribunal/
"In EF v AB [2015] IRLR 619, the EAT overturned the decision of the employment tribunal and granted a permanent restricted reporting order. The claimant had made allegations of sex discrimination and harassment against the CEO (EF), which included salacious allegations against the CEO’s wife (NP). The claimant was unsuccessful in his claims and EF and NP sought an order making permanent a temporary restricted reporting order that had been granted. Mrs Justice Slade concluded that EF and NP were entitled to a high degree of privacy in respect of the allegations about their sexual conduct and that their Article 8 rights (privacy) were therefore engaged."

"The NLRA guarantees the right of employees to organize into unions, engage in collective bargaining, and take collective action. Under the NLRA, workers have the right to self-organization, to form, join, or assist labor organizations, to bargain collectively through representatives of their own choosing, and to engage in other concerted activities for the purpose of collective bargaining or other mutual aid and protection.
[...]
The First Amendment of the Constitution, which guarantees the freedom of speech, assembly, and petition, also guarantees freedom of association and is recognized by U.S. courts as a fundamental right. The freedom of association encompasses the right of the group to take collective action to pursue the interests of its members. For this obvious reason, the constitutionally protected freedom of association includes unionization and union-related activities."

These include false arrest and imprisonment, perjury, police brutality and corruption, racial profiling, falsification of evidence, fraud, coercion or torture to force a confession, abuse of authority, physical or sexual assault, and spoliation of evidence.

The most common misconduct claims are:

-Harassment: A successful police harassment lawsuit requires evidence of a pattern of behavior on the part of the police officer or department. Harassment can include a variety of behavior on the part of the police, including illegally spying or surveilling, racial profiling, or making sexist, racist, or homophobic comments.

-Discrimination: Like harassment, there must be proof that the behavior is part of a pattern. Discrimination might be on the basis of race, ethnicity, gender, sex, or sexual orientation."

One might conjecture that supply could also decrease given that the state will want to raise taxes from legalized prostitution, whereas illegal prostitution, by definition, does not entail payment of taxes. "The Value Added Tax, or VAT, in the European Union is a general, broadly based consumption tax assessed on the value added to goods and services. It applies more or less to all goods and services that are bought and sold for use or consumption in the European Union. Thus, goods which are sold for export or services which are sold to customers abroad are normally not subject to VAT. Conversely imports are taxed to keep the system fair for EU producers so that they can compete on equal terms on the European market with suppliers situated outside the Union."
[344] https://www.investopedia.com/terms/s/substitution-effect.asp
"In general, when the price of a product or service increases but the buyer's income stays the same, the substitution effect kicks in. This is not only evident in consumer behavior. For example, a manufacturer faced with a price hike for an essential component from a domestic supplier may switch to a cheaper version produced by a foreign competitor.

How, then, does any company get away with increasing its price? In addition to the substitution effect, there's the income effect—some of its customers may be enjoying an increase in spending power and be willing to buy a pricier product. A company's success in repricing its product is determined in part by how much of the substitution effect is offset by the income effect."
[345] https://saylordotorg.github.io/text_the-business-ethics-workshop/s17-02-three-theories-of-corporate-so.html
"The best companies have been those generating the highest sales, gaining the most customers, and clearing the largest profits. As for ethical questions, they’ve been arranged around the basic obligation to represent the owners’ central interest, which presumably is to profit from their investment. Consequently, the field of business ethics has mainly concerned conflicts and dilemmas erupting inside the company as people try to work together to win in the very competitive economic world. The idea of corporate social responsibility—along with the related ideas of the triple bottom line and stakeholder theory—opens a different kind of business ethics. Morality in the economic world is now about corporate directors sensing and responding to a broad range of obligations, ones extending through the town where the business is located and then out into surrounding communities and through society generally."

"Thomas Hobbes was an English political philosopher that believed mankind was naturally wicked and selfish. He believed nations, like people, were selfishly motivated to gain power and wealth. Therefore governments were created to protect people from themselves. This idea became known as the social contract theory.

Thomas Hobbes was one of the first philosophers to theorize on the social contract. The theory suggests that man without government is in a "state of nature" where the individual is at risk of losing life, liberty, and property. Without a government to protect the individual, life is “nasty, brutal, and short.” With a social contract the people surrender power to the state for protection from others who would do harm."
[378] https://ethics.org.au/ethics-explainer-the-harm-principle/
"The harm principle says people should be free to act however they wish unless their actions cause harm to somebody else.

The principle is a central tenet of the political philosophy known as liberalism and was first proposed by English philosopher John Stuart Mill.

The harm principle is not designed to guide the actions of individuals but to restrict the scope of criminal law and government restrictions of personal liberty."

[398] https://ethics.org.au/ethics-explainer-moral-relativism/
"Moral relativism is the idea that there are no absolute rules to determine whether something is right or wrong. Unlike moral absolutists, moral relativists argue that good and bad are relative concepts – whether something is considered right or wrong can change depending on opinion, social context, culture or a number of other factors.

Moral relativists argue that there is more than one valid system of morality. A quick glance around the world or through history will reveal that no matter what we happen to believe is morally right and wrong, there is at least one person or culture that believes differently, and holds their belief with as much conviction as we do.

This existence of widespread moral diversity throughout history, between cultures and even within cultures, has led some philosophers to argue that morality is not absolute, but rather that there might be many valid moral systems: that morality is relative."

"The Investment Model of Relationships suggests that relationships are like investments: the more you put in, the more you get out. According to this model, the success of a relationship is determined by the level of investment that each partner puts in, as well as the various costs and benefits associated with that investment. In other words, the more you invest in your partner and your relationship, the more likely you are to be satisfied and committed to that relationship, and the less likely you are to stray or seek alternative options.
[...]
Another factor that can influence the success of a relationship according to the Investment Model is the presence of alternatives. If a partner perceives that there are many attractive alternatives to their current relationship, they may be less likely to invest in that relationship and more likely to seek out those alternatives."

An interest is compelling when it is essential or necessary rather than a matter of choice, preference, or discretion.

Regulation vital to the protection of public health and safety, including the regulation of violent crime, the requirements of national security and military necessity, and respect for fundamental rights are examples of compelling governmental interests"

Enthusiastic consent removes all the awkwardness and fear of uncertainty and replaces it with unmistakable hotness of getting — and giving — a “Hell yes!” It’s a whole-body experience that involves paying attention to one’s partner and checking in with their physical and emotional cues. Enthusiastic consent requires communication, either verbal or physical, or both. It can include nodding, making eye contact, smiling, looking relaxed and excited, kissing and touching the other person back, breathing heavily, moaning and just outrightly saying one likes what is being done to them with statements like “Don’t stop,” or “Oh, that feels so good.”"

[417] https://www.reeds.co.uk/insight/stealthing-and-conditional-consent/
"Conditional Consent is when consent is given under imposed conditions. If an act falls outside of the given conditions, then the act is non-consensual and goes against the consent given.

The Sexual Offences Act 2003, at Section 74, requires that a person ‘agree by choice, and has the freedom and capacity to make that choice’.

Under UK law consent is specific and is required for each sexual act. In some situations it can be argued that by lying or deceitful action a person can deprive someone of making a full and free choice. For example, by removing a condom without consent or knowledge, the individual deprives the person from making a choice. The person may not have consented to the sexual encounter if they had been aware of the full facts.

In English and Welsh law this is known as ‘conditional consent’ in sexual encounters."
[418] https://system.suny.edu/sexual-violence-prevention-workgroup/policies/affirmative-consent/
"Affirmative consent is a knowing, voluntary, and mutual decision among all participants to engage in sexual activity. Consent can be given by words or actions, as long as those words or actions create clear permission regarding willingness to engage in the sexual activity. Silence or lack of resistance, in and of itself, does not demonstrate consent. The definition of consent does not vary based upon a participant's sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, or gender expression."

[421] https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2022/03/17/sex-ethics-rethinking-consent-culture/
"Even the qualified versions of consent — the “affirmative,” the “enthusiastic” — have the lowest possible standard as their working assumption: “Did I get permission, so that my actions are not statedly against this person’s will?” The new adjectives are often understood as simply shifting the goal posts — rather than stopping when your partner says “no,” you just have to get them to say “yes” in the right way.

The problem with all this is that consent is a legal criterion, not an ethical one. It doesn’t tell us how we should treat each other as an interaction continues. It doesn’t provide a good road map should something go off the rails. And it suggests that individual actions — “ask for consent,” “speak your mind,” “be more forceful in saying yes or no” — are enough to preempt the misunderstandings and hurt that can come with physical intimacy.

Too often, they’re just not."

[426] https://www.zbw.eu/econis-archiv/bitstream/11159/331868/1/EBP07552807X_0.pdf
"Smith argues that duress responds to two moral concerns. First, contract law should not serve as a tool for extortion. Second, promisors should not be held responsible for obligations that they did not really choose. Regarding the former, a promisee who threatens to infringe on a promisor’s rights thereby wrongs the promisor for much the same reason that actually doing the threatened action would wrong the promisor. If contract law enforces contracts that result from threats, it undermines the violated rights. [...] For a promisee’s prediction to render the promisor’s consent morally inert, the promisee must actually have had control over the alternatives to contracting and indicate that she will exert such control in a way that violates the promisor’s rights. Similarly, if the promisor could have easily avoided the threatened action, the threat is effectively idle and so no wrong has truly been done."

[438] https://scholarship.law.slu.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1161&context=lj
"(1) A has sex with B because B pays A to have sex with B.

(2) A has sex with B because a third party pays A and B to have sex with
each other in order to make a pornographic picture or film.

If (1) is true, then A has committed the crime of prostitution. If (2) is true,
then (in many jurisdictions) A has not committed the crime of prostitution. 

This seems strange. A has engaged in exactly the same physical movements in both cases, with the same intent—A had sex with B intentionally. In both cases, A has done so for money. In neither case is there any reason to think A was motivated by desire or arousal or personal pleasure of any sort; in neither case is there any reason to think A felt love or affection or any sort of personal connection to B. In both cases, A’s conduct might be described as acting or as a performance."
